U_{\mathrm{plug}}
, with the ordinary U-statistic as well as the semi-supervised variance estimator introduced by
Zhang and Bradic, 2022
. The latter approach is referred to as ZB and the form of the estimator is given in equation (S9) of their supplementary material. Like our cross-fit estimator, the ZB estimator relies on cross-fitting as well as regression estimators. To ensure a fair comparison, we use two-fold cross-fitting for both
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
and ZB estimator, and consider either XGBoost or random forest regression with default parameters. The kernel for variance estimation is
\ell(y_{1},y_{2})=(y_{1}-y_{2})^{2}/2
and its conditional expectation is given as
\ell_{1}(y)=y^{2}/2-y\mathbb{E}(Y)+\mathbb{E}(Y^{2})/2
. In our simulations, we estimate
\ell_{1}(y)
as
\widehat{\ell}_{1}(y)=y^{2}/2-y\widehat{\mu}_{1}+\widehat{\mu}_{2}/2
where
\widehat{\mu}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mu}_{2}
are the first and second moments of the empirical distribution of
Y
. We then regress
\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)
on
X
to form
\widehat{f}
for
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
and
\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}
for
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
. It is worth noting that in
Section

3.1
, we introduce additional splits to construct
\widehat{\ell}_{1}
for theoretical analysis. This additional layer of random sources, however, does not lead to a significant improvement in the empirical performance of the final estimator. We therefore opt for a simpler approach using
{\widehat{\ell}}_{1}
formed without additional splitting in our simulation studies.

Figure 1:
Comparing MSE ratios for different
m
values: (a) The left panel indicates that the ZB estimator performs better than
\{U_{\mathrm{cross}},U_{\mathrm{plug}}\}
in Model 1 (linear additive model). (b) Conversely, the right panel demonstrates that the ZB estimator performs less effectively than
\{U_{\mathrm{cross}},U_{\mathrm{plug}}\}
in Model 2 (non-linear model). In all scenarios, the semi-supervised estimators consistently outperform
U
, especially when
m
is large. See
Section

8.1
for details.

The performance of the considered estimators is evaluated under the following two scenarios with
n=1000
, while varying the value of
m
from
10
to
100000
.

1.

Model 1
: Let
X=(X^{(1)},\ldots,X^{(10)})^{\top}\sim N(0,\bm{I}_{10})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
\varepsilon\sim N(0,1)
and
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{5}X^{(i)}+0.3\varepsilon
where
\bm{I}_{p}
is the
p\times p
identity matrix, and
X,\varepsilon
are mutually independent.

2.

Model 2
: Let
X=(X^{(1)},\ldots,X^{(10)})^{\top}\sim N(0,\bm{I}_{10})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
\varepsilon\sim N(0,1)
,
\delta\in\{-1,+1\}
with equal probability and
Y=\delta\sqrt{(X^{(1)})^{2}+(X^{(2)})^{2}+0.3^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}
where
X,\varepsilon,\delta
are mutually independent.

In
Figure

1
, we display the MSE ratio, which is computed as the MSE of the ordinary U-statistic,
U
, divided by the MSE of the estimator among
\{\mathrm{ZB},U_{\mathrm{cross}},U_{\mathrm{plug}}\}
. Consequently, when this ratio exceeds one, it indicates that the considered semi-supervised estimator is more efficient than
U
.
Figure

1
showcases that all of
\{\mathrm{ZB},U_{\mathrm{cross}},U_{\mathrm{plug}}\}
are more efficient than
U
in both scenarios. Within the semi-supervised estimators, the ZB estimator performs better than our approaches for the linear additive model as shown in the left panel of
Figure

1
. Conversely, the right panel of
Figure

1
tells a different story that the semi-supervised U-statistics outperform the ZB estimator in the non-linear model. These empirical results do not contradict our minimax optimality result, which focuses on the worst-case risk for a specific model, allowing for the possibility of more efficient estimators in different settings.
We also remark that the choice of regressors between XGBoost and random forest does not significantly impact the results, and
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
and
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
perform comparably in both scenarios.

Figure 2:
Comparing MSE ratios for different mean values (
\mu
): (a) The left panel indicates that
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
performs better than both
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
and
U
when
\mu
is close to zero, whereas it performs comparable to
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
when
\mu
is far away from zero. This observation applies to both regression methods and highlights the adaptive property of
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
. (b) The right panel displays a similar pattern to the left panel, while the estimator based on least squares regression shows no gain over
U
due to model misspecification. See
Section

8.2
for details.

8.2
Estimation of
\mu^{2}

Next we revisit the setting in
Section

7.2
to demonstrate the adaptive property of
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
in estimating
\mu^{2}
. Recall that the construction of
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
relies on estimators
\widehat{\ell}_{1}
and
\widehat{\ell}_{2}
. To this end, we follow the approach described in (
11
), employing the least squares linear regression and
k
-nearest neighbor regression with
k=5
to compute
\widehat{\ell}_{1}
and
\widehat{\ell}_{2}
as outlined in (
11
). To evaluate the performance, we focus on two scenarios with
n=500
and
m=10000
described below.

1.

Model 1
: Let
X=(X^{(1)},\ldots,X^{(4)})^{\top}\sim N(0,\Sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
where
\Sigma=0.3\bm{I}_{4}+0.7\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}
,
\varepsilon\sim N(0,1)
and
Y=\mu+X^{(1)}+X^{(2)}+0.3\varepsilon
where
\bm{1}
is a
p
-dimensional vector of ones.

2.

Model 2
: Let
X=(X^{(1)},\ldots,X^{(4)})^{\top}\sim N(0,\Sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
where
\Sigma=0.3\bm{I}_{4}+0.7\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}
,
\varepsilon\sim N(0,1)
and
Y=\mu+\sin(5X^{(1)})+\sin(3X^{(2)})+0.3\varepsilon
.

In
Figure

2
, we show the ratio of the MSEs for the ordinary U-statistic
U
and the proposed adaptive estimator
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
. As before, a value greater than one indicates that
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
is more efficient than
U
. For comparisons, we also consider
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
with
\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}
computed by regressing
\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)
on
X
using either the least squares method or the
5
-nearest neighbor method where we take
\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)=\mu Y
for simplicity.

The left panel of
Figure

2
highlights that
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
significantly reduces the MSE over both
U
and
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
when
\mu
is close to zero. Moreover,
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
and
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
perform comparably as
\mu
deviates from zero, both consistently maintaining smaller errors than
U
. This observation remains the same for both least squares and nearest neighbor regression. In contrast, the right panel of
Figure

2
demonstrates that the estimator based on the least square regression has no gain over
U
due to the non-linear nature of the underlying model. On the other hand, the estimator based on the nearest neighbor method tells a consistent story as in the left panel of
Figure

2
. This observation confirms the adaptive property of
U_{\mathrm{adapt}}
and underscores the significant role played by estimators
\widehat{\ell}_{1}
and
\widehat{\ell}_{2}
in estimation performance.

8.3
Semi-Supervised Kendall’s
\tau

As an application of the proposed framework, we introduce semi-supervised Kendall’s
\tau
tests for statistical independence and compare its performance with the classical approach. Given a set of i.i.d. bivariate random vectors
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}:=\{(V_{i},W_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, Kendall’s
\tau
measures the similarity between
V_{i}
’s and
W_{i}
’s by counting the number of concordant and discordant pairs. The test statistic of Kendall’s
\tau
test can be represented as a U-statistic with the bivariate kernel
\ell(y_{1},y_{2})=\mathrm{sign}(v_{1}-v_{2})\,\mathrm{sign}(w_{1}-w_{2})
as detailed below:

\displaystyle\tau=\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\sum_{(n,2)}\mathrm{sign}(V_{i}-V_{j})\,\mathrm{sign}(W_{i}-W_{j}).

The properties of Kendall’s
\tau
have been well-established in the literature. For example, under the null hypothesis of independence for continuous data,
\tau
is distribution-free, converging to a Normal distribution as
\sqrt{n}\tau\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,4/9)

(e.g.,
van der Vaart, 2000
, page 164)
. This asymptotic result leads to a simple decision rule for independence testing, which rejects the null hypothesis when
3\sqrt{n}|\tau|/2>z_{1-\alpha/2}
where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
denotes the
1-\alpha/2
quantile of
N(0,1)
.

Our goal is to adapt
\tau
to semi-supervised settings, utilizing both the labeled dataset
\mathcal{D}_{XY}
of size
n
as well as the unlabeled dataset
\mathcal{D}_{X}
of size
m
. First, as shown in
Lee, 1990
, the conditional expectation
\ell_{1}(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}\{\ell(Y_{1},Y_{2})\,|\,Y_{2}=\cdot\}
can be computed as

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(y)=\ell_{1}\{(v,w)\}=\{1-2F_{V}(v)\}\{1-2F_{W}(w)\}+4\{F_{V,W}(v,w)-F_{V}(v)F_{W}(w)\},

where
F_{V}
and
F_{W}
denote the cumulative distribution function of
V
and
W
, respectively, and
F_{V,W}
represents the bivariate cumulative distribution function of
(V,W)
. As an initial step to form
\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}
and
\widehat{f}
for
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
and
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
, respectively, we estimate
\ell_{1}
by replacing
F_{V}
,
F_{W}
and
F_{V,W}
with the corresponding empirical cumulative distributions. We then regress the resulting estimator
\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)
on
X
to construct
\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}
and
\widehat{f}
using either XGBoost or random forest. Next, we reject the null hypothesis when
\sqrt{n}|U_{\mathrm{cross}}|>z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\smash[b]{\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}}}

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}=\frac{4}{9}+\frac{4m}{n+m}\Biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Biggl(\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{j})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{j})\}\Biggr]\Biggr)^{2}-\frac{1}{9}\Biggr\}.

This variance estimate is formulated based on our discussion in
Section

A.1
and the fact that
\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\}=1/9
under the null hypothesis. The test based on
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
is similarly defined by replacing
\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}
with
\widehat{f}
trained without sample splitting.

Figure 3:
Type I error and power results for Kendall’s
\tau
experiments with
m=50000
: (a) The left panel displays estimated type I error rates of Kendall’s
\tau
and semi-supervised counterparts at
\alpha=0.05
by varying the sample size. (b) The right panel shows the estimated power of the considered tests by changing the correlation parameter
\rho
with
n=5000
. These results indicate that the semi-supervised tests outperform classical Kendall’s
\tau
in terms of power, while the approach using
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
is anti-conservative in small sample scenarios. See
Section

8.3
for details.

To evaluate the performance of the resulting tests, we generate covariates
X=(X^{(1)},X^{(2)})^{\top}\sim N(0,\Sigma)
where
\Sigma=(1-\rho)\bm{I}_{2}+\rho\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}
. The response variables are subsequently generated as
Y=(V,W)
where
V=X^{(1)}+0.05\varepsilon_{1}
,
W=X^{(2)}+0.05\varepsilon_{2}
and
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}N(0,1)
. In this setting, the correlation parameter
\rho
controls the dependence of
V
and
W
, leading to the null hypothesis when
\rho=0
. In
Figure

3
, we record the empirical type I error and power of the considered tests at a significance level of
\alpha=0.05
. Specifically, the left panel of
Figure

3
displays the type I error rates of the tests by changing
n
from
100
to
5000
, while fixing
m=50000
. The results reveal that the test based on
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
is overly anti-conservative when
n
is small, although its type I error converges to
\alpha
as
n
increases. On the other hand, both Kendall’s
\tau
test and the test based on
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
effectively maintain the type I error rate under control, with the latter test being slightly conservative when
n
is small. Moving on, the right panel of
Figure

3
displays the power of the considered tests by increasing the correlation parameter
\rho
, while fixing
n=5000
and
m=50000
. In this regime where all of the tests are well-calibrated, it is clear to see that the proposed semi-supervised methods outperform classical Kendall’s
\tau
by a substantial margin. Furthermore, there is no significant difference between
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
and
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
in their power performance for both approaches based on XGBoost and random forest. Nevertheless, we recommend using
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
in practice as it demonstrates more reliable control of the size across different sample sizes.

Figure 4:
Type I error and power results for experiments of Wilcoxon signed rank test with
m=50000
: (a) The left panel displays estimated type I error rates of Wilcoxon test and semi-supervised counterparts at
\alpha=0.05
by varying the sample size. (b) The right panel shows the estimated power of the considered tests by changing the correlation parameter
\mu
with
n=2500
. These results indicate that the semi-supervised tests outperform classical Wilcoxon test in terms of power, while the approach using
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
is anti-conservative in small sample scenarios. See
Section

8.4
for details.

8.4
Semi-Supervised Wilcoxon Signed Rank Test

We next build upon our framework and introduce the semi-supervised Wilcoxon signed rank test. Let
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be drawn i.i.d. from a continuous distribution, and denote
R_{i}
be the rank of
|Y_{i}|
for each
i\in[n]
. The classical Wilcoxon signed rank test uses the signed-rank sum as a test statistic, which can be written as
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{sign}(Y_{i})R_{i}=n(n-1)U^{(1)}+2nU^{(2)}-n(n+1)
where

\displaystyle U^{(1)}=\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\sum_{(n,2)}\mathds{1}(Y_{i}+Y_{j}>0)\quad\text{and}\quad U^{(2)}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(Y_{i}>0).

Since the asymptotic behavior of the Wilcoxon test statistic is determined by
U^{(1)}

(e.g.,
van der Vaart, 2000
, page 183)
, we consider semi-supervised U-statistics with the kernel
\ell(y_{1},y_{2})=\mathds{1}(y_{1}+y_{2}>0)
, and introduce tests calibrated by Normal approximations. The considered algorithms are essentially the same as before in
Section

8.3
for Kendall’s
\tau
except that the kernel is now
\ell(y_{1},y_{2})=\mathds{1}(y_{1}+y_{2}>0)
and the corresponding
\ell_{1}
is given as
\ell_{1}(y)=1-F_{Y}(-y)
where
F_{Y}
is the cumulative distribution function of
Y
. We again estimate
\ell_{1}
by replacing
F_{Y}
with the empirical cumulative distribution, and form
\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}
by regressing the estimated
\ell_{1}(Y)
on
X
based on either XGBoost or random forest. We then compute
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
and reject the null hypothesis
H_{0}:\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}+Y_{2}>0)=1/2
if
\sqrt{n}|U_{\mathrm{cross}}-1/2|>z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\smash[b]{\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}}}

where

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}=\frac{1}{3}+\frac{4m}{n+m}\Biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Biggl(\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{j})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{j})\}\Biggr]\Biggr)^{2}-\frac{1}{12}\Biggr\}.

This variance estimate is based on the one suggested in
Section

A.1
along with the fact that
\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\}=1/12
under the null hypothesis. The test based on
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
is similarly defined by training
\widehat{f}
without sample splitting.

In order to evaluate the performance, we consider model 1 in
Section

8.2
with a slight modification to amplify the problem signal. Specifically, let
X=(X^{(1)},\ldots,X^{(4)})^{\top}\sim N(0,\Sigma)
where
\Sigma=0.3\bm{I}_{4}+0.7\bm{1}\bm{1}^{\top}
and set
Y=\mu+X^{(1)}+X^{(2)}+0.05\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon\sim N(0,1)
. We remark that the location parameter
\mu
controls the problem signal, resulting in the null hypothesis when
\mu=0
.

The simulation results are recorded in
Figure

4
where we set
\alpha=0.05
and
m=50000
. The left panel displays the type I error rates of the considered tests under the null hypothesis by changing
n
, whereas the right panel shows the power results simulated by changing
\mu
. Overall, we observe similar patterns shown in
Figure

3
for Kendall’s
\tau
where the semi-supervised approaches substantially improve the power of the classical Wilcoxon test. In terms of type I error control, the test based on
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
is highly miscalibrated for small
n
, which suggests
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
would be preferable in practice involving limited sample sizes.

9
Discussion

In this work, we introduced semi-supervised U-statistics that improve classical U-statistics by leveraging unlabeled data. Equipped with the cross-fitting principle, the proposed approach can effectively integrate a variety of powerful prediction tools from the literature and demonstrates notable efficiency gains over the classical approach under minimal assumptions. For non-degenerate kernels, we established conditions ensuring the asymptotic Normality of the proposed semi-supervised estimators and quantified finite-sample deviations using Berry–Esseen bounds. We further showed that the proposed estimators are asymptotically efficient by establishing minimax lower bounds in semi-supervised settings. Focusing on U-statistics with bivariate kernels, we introduced an approach adaptive to the degeneracy of kernels. Our findings reveal that this refined method improves upon the classical U-statistic across all degeneracy regimes, and achieves optimal minimax bounds in certain scenarios.

Our work opens up several fruitful avenues for future work. One potential direction is to expand our results to incorporate other forms of U-statistics, such as
k
-sample U-statistics and weighted U-statistics. These extensions would broaden the scope of the proposed framework, allowing us to explore other important statistical problems within semi-supervised settings. It would also be interesting to mitigate the computational burden of the proposed procedure associated with multiple summations. For instance, one might consider averaging kernels over a selected subset of data pairs, known as incomplete U-statistics
(
Blom, 1976
;
Lee, 1990
;
Schrab et al., 2022
)
. This alternative approach offers a trade-off between computational costs and efficiency, depending on the chosen subset. We leave it as future work to incorporate incomplete U-statistics into our semi-supervised framework and explore their properties in detail. Another important direction for future work is to delve deeper into adaptive results in
Section

7
, and extend these to higher-order kernels. These results would directly benefit numerous inference procedures
(
Kim et al., 2020
;
Kim et al., 2022
, e.g.,)
, which are based on degenerate U-statistics. Lastly, our work inspires a more systematic investigation of the connection between the semi-supervised framework and the missing data framework. This connection would enable us to exchange tools and findings developed within distinct frameworks, ultimately enhancing our ability to address complex problems in semi-supervised learning and missing data scenarios.

Acknowledgments.

The authors are grateful to Edward H. Kennedy for helpful discussions. IK thanks Gyumin Lee for his careful proofreading and suggestions.

Supplementary material

This supplementary material includes additional results as well as proofs of the main results omitted due to space limitations.

Organization.

The supplementary material is organized as follows. In
Appendix

A
, we present additional results, including variance estimation of semi-supervised U-statistics (
Section

A.1
), the Berry–Esseen bound for the high-dimensional least squares estimator (
Section

A.2
), connections between random-
N
sampling and fixed-
N
sampling (
Section

A.3
), and the minimax lower bound for mean estimation (
Section

A.4
).
Appendix

B
contains auxiliary lemmas that are used to prove the main results of this work. In
Appendix

C
, we collect the proofs of the results in the main text, whereas the proofs of additional results in
Appendix

A
are provided in
Appendix

D
.

Notation.

In addition to the notation introduced in the main text, we make use of another set of notation throughout this supplementary material. Let
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1},(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be two sequences of real numbers. As convention, we often write
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
to denote that there exists a positive constant
C
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
. For a positive integer
d
, the symbol
\bm{I}_{d}
represents the
d\times d
identity matrix. We use
C,C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
to denote some generic positive constants whose value may vary in different places.

Appendix A
Additional Results

In this section, we collect several additional results that complement those in the main text.

A.1
Variance Estimation

This subsection presents a consistent estimator of
\Lambda_{n,m,f}
in (
5
), which can be used to construct a confidence interval or conduct hypothesis testing for
\psi
together with the asymptotic Normality of
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
. While the proposed estimator can be applied to a general kernel
\ell
, one can design simpler and potentially more efficient variance estimators by taking into account a specific structure of
\ell
as demonstrated in
Section

8.3
and
Section

8.4
.

There are two terms in
\Lambda_{n,m,f}
that we need to estimate, namely
\sigma^{2}:=\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\}
and
\tau_{f}:=\mathrm{Var}\{f(X)\}-2\mathrm{Cov}\{f(X),\psi_{1}(X)\}
. To estimate the first term
\sigma^{2}
, we consider the Jackknife estimator
(
Arvesen, 1969
)
. To explain, denote the U-statistic computed from a sample of size
n-1
excluding
Y_{i}
as

\displaystyle U^{(i)}=\binom{n-1}{r}^{-1}\sum_{(n,r)\setminus i}\ell(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{r}}),

where the summation is taken over all permutations of
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
chosen from
[n]\!\setminus\!\{i\}
. Then the Jackknife estimator of
\sigma^{2}
is given as

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{(n-1)}{r^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(U^{(i)}-U\bigr)^{2}.

For the second term
\tau_{f}
, it is easier to work with another expression for
\tau_{f}=\mathrm{Var}\{f(X)-\ell_{1}(Y)\}-\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\}
, which can be estimated by

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}_{f}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Biggl(\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\Biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{j})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{j})\}\Biggr]\Biggr)^{2}-\widehat{\sigma}^{2},

where
\widehat{\ell}_{1}
is defined as in (
7
) but based on
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. The following corollary establishes the asymptotic Normality of
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
when
\Lambda_{n,m,f}
is replaced by its estimator
\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}:=r^{2}\widehat{\sigma}^{2}+r^{2}m\widehat{\tau}_{f}/(n+m)
. In fact,
Corollary

3
holds when
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}
and
\widehat{\tau}_{f}
are replaced with any consistent estimators of
\sigma^{2}
and
\tau_{f}
.

Corollary 3
.

Under the same conditions in
Theorem

1
, the semi-supervised U-statistic
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
scaled by
\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n}(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1)\quad\text{as $n\rightarrow\infty$.}

The proof of
Corollary

3
can be found in
Section

D.1
.

A.2
High-dimensional Least Squares Estimator

In this subsection, we explore a Berry–Esseen bound for
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
tailored to least squares estimators as in
Zhang et al., 2019
. For simplicity, we focus on the problem of mean estimation by setting
\ell(y)=y
. To delineate, we use the notation
\vec{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
to denote
\vec{X}^{\top}=(1,X^{\top})
and write the coefficients of the best linear predictor of
Y
given
\vec{X}
as

\displaystyle\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{(2)})^{\top}=\argmin_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}}\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\bigl(Y-\vec{X}^{\top}\gamma\bigr)^{2}\bigr\},

where
\beta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta_{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We then set the target assistant-function
f
as
f(x):=x^{\top}\beta_{(2)}
and use its estimates
\widehat{f}_{1}
and
\widehat{f}_{2}
in the construction of
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
. A natural estimator of
f
is the least squares estimator. Based on
\mathcal{D}_{XY,1}
of size
\lfloor n/2\rfloor:=n_{0}
, we compute the design matrix

\displaystyle\vec{\bm{X}}=\begin{bmatrix}\vec{X}_{1}^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\vec{X}_{n_{0}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1&X_{11}&X_{12}&\cdots&X_{1d}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
1&X_{n_{0}1}&X_{n_{0}2}&\cdots&X_{n_{0}d}\end{bmatrix}

and denote the vector of response variables as
\bm{Y}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n_{0}})^{\top}
. Then the least squares estimator of
f
is given as
\widehat{f}_{1}(x)=x^{\top}\widehat{\beta}_{(2)}
where
\widehat{\beta}=(\widehat{\beta}_{1},\widehat{\beta}_{(2)})^{\top}:=(\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{X}})^{-1}\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\bm{Y}
. Similarly, we compute the least squares estimator
\widehat{f}_{2}
of
f
based on
\mathcal{D}_{XY,2}
. The resulting
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
has the following Berry–Esseen bound where
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
indicate some positive constants and
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|v\|_{2}=1\}
denotes the
d
-dimensional unit sphere. The proof of
Proposition

5
below is provided in
Section

D.2
.

Proposition 5
.

Let us denote
\mathbb{E}(X)=\mu
and
\mathrm{Var}(X)=\Sigma
. Define a random vector
Z=\Sigma^{-1/2}(X-\mu)
and assume that
K_{d}:=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathbb{E}(|v^{\top}Z|)>C_{1}
and
d/n\leq C_{2}K_{d}
. Moreover assume the following moment conditions: (i)
\mathbb{E}(|Y-\mu|^{3})<C_{3}
, (ii)
\mathbb{E}\{\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X)\}>C_{4}
, (iii)
\mathbb{E}\{|\beta_{(2)}^{\top}(X-\mu)|^{3}\}<C_{5}
and (iv)
\max_{1\leq i\leq d+1}\mathbb{E}\{\vec{X}_{(i)}^{2}(Y-\vec{X}^{\top}\beta)^{2}\}<C_{6}
where
\vec{X}_{(i)}
denotes the
i
th component of
\vec{X}
. Let
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
be the semi-supervised U-statistic using the least squares estimators described above. Then there exists a constant
C
depending on
C_{1},\ldots,C_{6}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\biggl\{\frac{\sqrt{n}(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}\leq t\biggr\}-\Phi(t)\bigg|\leq C\biggl(\frac{d}{n}\biggr)^{1/3},

where
\Lambda_{n,m,f}
is defined in (
5
) with
r=1
,
\ell_{1}(Y)=Y
and
\psi_{1}(X)=\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)
.

Proposition

5
shows that
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
using the least squares estimator is asymptotically Normal when
d/n\rightarrow 0
under moment conditions. These moment conditions are weaker than the finite fourth moment condition considered in
Zhang et al., 2019
for their Berry–Esseen bound. One non-trivial assumption, on the other hand, is
\mathbb{E}\{|\beta_{(2)}^{\top}(X-\mu)|^{3}\}<C_{5}
. While we do not assume linearity, if
\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)=\beta_{(2)}^{\top}X
, then this assumption holds under the finite third moment of
Y
. Alternatively, when
Y
is bounded, it can be shown that
\mathbb{E}\{|\beta_{(2)}^{\top}(X-\mu)|^{3}\}\emph{}
is also bounded without the linearity assumption.

Proposition

5
may not be directly comparable to the Berry–Esseen bound in
Zhang et al., 2019
given that they consider a plug-in estimator. Nevertheless the bound in
Proposition

5
converges faster than the bound obtained in
Zhang et al., 2019
, which has the
n^{1/4}
-rate in a fixed dimensional setting.

A.3
Random-
N
Sampling versus Fixed-
N
Sampling

As explained in the main text, the missing data problem works on the setting where triplets
\{(X_{i},\delta_{i}Y_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n+m}
are i.i.d. drawn from the joint distribution of
(X,\delta Y,\delta)
where
\delta\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\varrho_{n})
. While the form of the resulting dataset may be identical to the one obtained under the semi-supervised framework, the joint distribution of
\{(X_{i},\delta_{i}Y_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n+m}
is not the same. In particular, the number of labeled samples
N:=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\delta_{i}
is a predetermined number in the semi-supervised setting, whereas it is a random variable in the missing data framework. Analyzing the missing data framework typically requires the positivity assumption, that is,
\varrho_{n}:=n/(n+m)\rightarrow\varrho\in(0,1)
, which excludes important cases where
m
is either significantly smaller or larger than
n
. By contrast, our semi-supervised framework allows
\varrho_{n}
to approach either
0
or
1
, and a significant portion of our results do not even require the convergence of
\varrho_{n}
. Nevertheless, these two sampling schemes are closely connected, and the goal of this subsection is to present their connection in terms of minimax risks. To fix the terminology, we simply call the sampling scheme with random missing indicators as
random-
N
sampling
, whereas the sampling scheme with a fixed number of
N=n
as
fixed-
N
sampling
.

As mentioned in the main text, the i.i.d. nature of random-
N
sampling simplifies the analysis and allows us to employ well-established tools for lower bounds from semi-parametric statistics, such as the local asymptotic minimax (LAM) theorem
(e.g.,
van der Vaart, 2000
, Theorem 25.21)
. The key idea is that if a lower bound holds under random-
N
sampling, it might similarly apply to fixed-
N
sampling, especially when the number of labeled dataset
N=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\delta_{i}
tightly concentrates around
n
. We build on this intuition and make their connection concrete in
Proposition

6
and
Corollary

4
.

Illustration.

To demonstrate the idea further, suppose that our aim is to return an estimate of the mean parameter, which can be expressed as
\psi=\mathbb{E}(Y)=\mathbb{E}\{\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X,\delta=1)\}
. It is well-known that
(e.g.,
Kennedy, 2022
, Example 2)
, the efficient influence function of
\psi
is given as

\displaystyle\varphi(X,\delta Y,\delta):=\frac{\mathds{1}(\delta=1)}{\mathbb{P}(\delta=1\,|\,X)}\{Y-\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X,\delta=1)\}+\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X,\delta=1)-\mathbb{E}(Y).

Under positivity (i.e.,
\varrho>0
) and missing completely at random assumptions, the variance of
\varphi
can be computed as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\{\varphi(X,\delta Y,\delta)\}=\mathrm{Var}\{\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X,\delta=1)\}+\varrho^{-1}\mathbb{E}\{\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X,\delta=1)\}.

The LAM theorem asserts that the asymptotic lower bound for the minimax squared
L_{2}
risk, scaled by
n+m
, is given as
\mathrm{Var}_{P}\{\varphi(X,\delta Y,\delta)\}
. This lower bound is established by considering the worst-case scenario within a neighborhood around the distribution
P
. We refer to
van der Vaart, 2000
for a precise statement. This local asymptotic lower bound partly recovers the global minimax lower bound for semi-supervised mean estimation in
Zhang et al., 2019
, which is also recalled in
Proposition

7
. However, in general cases, we cannot directly translate this lower bound result to fixed-
N
sampling without further assumptions. The following example demonstrates this point.

Example 3
.

Suppose that we observe i.i.d. triplets
\{(X_{i},\delta_{i},\delta_{i}Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n+m}
and let
A=\{\delta_{1}=\cdots=\delta_{n+m}=0\}
with
\mathbb{P}(A)>0
. Then a bias-variance trade-off yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{(\widehat{\psi}-\psi)^{2}\}~\geq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{(\widehat{\psi}-\psi)^{2}\mathds{1}(A)\}=\{\mathrm{Var}(\widehat{\psi}\,|\,A)+\{\mathbb{E}(\widehat{\psi}\,|\,A)-\psi\}^{2}\}\mathbb{P}(A)

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\{\mathbb{E}(\widehat{\psi}\,|\,A)-\psi\}^{2}\mathbb{P}(A).

Under the event
A
, we only observe
X
values and so
\mathbb{E}(\widehat{\psi}\,|\,A)
contains no information of
\psi
whenever
X
and
Y
are independent. By treating
\mathbb{E}(\widehat{\psi}\,|\,A)
as a constant, the lower bound becomes infinite if the parameter space for
\psi
is unbounded. On the other hand, the risk under fixed-
N
sampling, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\{(\widehat{\psi}-\psi)^{2}\,|\,\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\delta_{i}=n\}
, does not suffer from the same issue. This demonstrates that the worst-case risk under random-
N
sampling can be infinite, while that under fixed-
N
sampling is finite.

The gap between the minimax risks under different sampling schemes arises because the risk function is unbounded in the above example. We show in
Corollary

4
that the minimax risks can be made asymptotically equivalent for bounded risk functions under regularity conditions. In fact,
Corollary

4
follows as a direct consequence of
Proposition

6
below, which establishes a non-asymptotic relationship between the unconditional and conditional minimax risks for some generic estimation problem.

Proposition 6
.

Given a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F})
equipped with a class of probability measures
\{P_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
of
(X,\delta Y,\delta)
and an action space
\widehat{\Theta}
, let
\mathcal{L}:\widehat{\Theta}\times\Theta\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be a loss function. Suppose that

(i)

The experiment is dominated, i.e., there exists some measure
\mu
such that
P_{\theta}\ll\mu
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.

(ii)

The action space
\widehat{\Theta}
is a locally compact topological space with a countable base (e.g., Euclidean space).

(iii)

For each
\theta\in\Theta
, the loss function
\mathcal{L}(\cdot,\theta)
is bounded below and the sublevel set
\{\widehat{\theta}:\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\leq a\}
is compact for each
a
.

(iv)

The missing indicator
\delta
follows
\delta\sim\mathrm{Bernoull}(\varrho)
with
\varrho=n/(n+m)
and it is independent of
X
and
Y
.

Consider the unconditional minimax risk
\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\}
where the expectation is taken over
\{(X_{i},\delta_{i}Y_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n+m}
i.i.d. copies of
(X,\delta Y,\delta)\sim P_{\theta}
, and denote
N=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\delta_{i}
. Then for any
q\in(1/2,1)
, the unconditional risk is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathsf{Risk}_{L,q}\leq\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\}\leq\mathsf{Risk}_{U,q}

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{Risk}_{L,q}:=\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor\}\times\bigl(1-e^{-n^{2q-1}/4}\bigr)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Risk}_{U,q}:=\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor\}+\biggl(\sup_{\widehat{\theta},\theta}[\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\}]^{1/2}+1\biggr)\times e^{-n^{2q-1}/4}.

The abstract conditions (i), (ii) and (iii) are imposed to apply the minimax theorem
(
Strasser, 1985
, Theorem 46.6)
under which the minimax risk equals the Bayes risk with a least favorable prior. As discussed in
Polyanskiy and Wu, 2023
, these conditions are mild and satisfied for general problems such as the one with the
L_{2}
risk defined on the Euclidean space that we consider in this paper. The proof of
Proposition

6
builds on the ideas that
N\sim\mathrm{Binomial}\bigl((n+m,n/(n+m)\bigr)
concentrates around
n
with high probability and the conditional risk of a (near)-optimal estimator exhibits monotonic behavior as a function of
N
. These ideas, combined with the fact that the unconditional risk can be expressed as a weighted average of conditional risks, establishes the desired bounds. The details can be found in
Section

D.3
. We remark that, as demonstrated in
Example

3
, the conditional and unconditional minimax risks can be significantly different when the loss function is unbounded over the parameter space. Therefore the term
\sup_{\widehat{\theta},\theta}[\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\}]^{1/2}
in the upper bound cannot be entirely negligible.

As a direct corollary of
Proposition

6
, the following result identifies sufficient conditions under which the conditional and unconditional risks are asymptotically equivalent.

Corollary 4
.

Consider the regularity conditions in
Proposition

6
on data-generating distributions and loss function. If we further assume that

(i)

The worst-case risk function
\sup_{\widehat{\theta},\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\}
is bounded above by some positive constant.

(ii)

The ratio of the minimax (conditional) risks satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n\{1+o(1)\}\rfloor\}}{\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=n\}}=1+o(1).

(iii)

Neither conditional nor unconditional minimax risks converge at a rate faster than exponential.

Then the conditional minimax risk and unconditional minimax risk are asymptotically equivalent as

\displaystyle\frac{\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\}}{\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=n\}}=1+o(1).

As we mentioned earlier, the bounded condition (i) is not entirely avoidable in view of
Example

3
. Condition (ii) requires that the conditional minimax risk is asymptotically continuous as a function of
N
. Alternatively, this condition (ii) can be replaced by a condition on the unconditional minimax risk. Specifically, if we consider
\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\}=h(\varrho)
as a function of the parameter
\varrho
for the missing indicator, condition (ii) can be replaced with
h(\varrho_{1,n})/h(\varrho_{2,n})=1+o(1)
whenever
\varrho_{1,n}/\varrho_{2,n}=1+o(1)
. The last condition (iii) concerning the convergence rate is mild and it is expected to be satisfied for almost all practical problems.

The asymptotic equivalence established in
Corollary

4
allows us to apply the LAM theorem to investigate the minimax risk under fixed-
N
sampling. However, in the argument of the LAM theorem, the positivity of
\varrho
is critical and it would take non-trivial effort to extend the result to incorporate a triangular array of distributions with varying
\varrho
. Therefore a direct translation from random-
N
sampling to fixed-
N
sampling yields a lower bound result limited to certain asymptotic regimes. In contrast, we take a direct approach to derive the lower bound results in the main text, specifically utilizing the van Trees inequality, and we avoid imposing an unnecessary restriction on
\varrho
.

A.4
Minimax Lower Bound for Mean Estimation

In this subsection, we briefly revisit the lower bound result for mean estimation in
Zhang et al., 2019
, and provide an alternative proof in
Section

D.4
through the van Trees inequality. We reprove this result merely to illustrate the versatility of the van Trees inequality in establishing minimax lower bounds under semi-supervised settings.

Proposition 7

(
Zhang et al., 2019
, Proposition 3)
.

Consider the mean estimation problem with
\psi=\mathbb{E}(Y)
. Let
\sigma_{X}^{2}
and
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
be some fixed positive numbers. Then for the class of distributions

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{mean}}=\big\{P_{XY}:Y=X+\varepsilon,\,X\sim N(\delta,\sigma_{X}^{2}),\,\varepsilon\sim N(c,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\ \text{where $X$ and $\varepsilon$ are independent}\big\},

the minimax risk is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\psi}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{mean}}}n\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl\{(\widehat{\psi}-\psi_{P})^{2}\bigr\}\geq\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\frac{n}{n+m}\sigma_{X}^{2}.

Moreover, it holds that
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{P}\{\mathrm{Var}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\}
and
\sigma_{X}^{2}=\mathrm{Var}_{P}\{\mathbb{E}_{P}(Y\,|\,X)\}
for any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{mean}}
.

We note that
Zhang et al., 2019
considers a larger class of distributions than
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{mean}}
but their main argument revolves around the distributions in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{mean}}
.
Zhang et al., 2019
prove
Proposition

7
using the well-known fact that a Bayes estimator with constant risk is minimax. In their construction, the key is to express the target parameter
\psi
as a function of other two parameters, namely
\delta
and
c
, and consider a scenario where the unlabeled data provide additional information of
\delta
but not
c
. This construction allows us to obtain the second term in the lower bound, which tends to zero as the size of unlabeled data
m
increases. We build on their construction and show the same result based on the van Trees inequality in
Section

D.4
.

Appendix B
Technical Lemmas

This section collects several technical lemmas. The first result displayed below is known as Stone’s theorem, which states conditions which guarantee the consistency of a linear smoother in terms of the MSPE. Given i.i.d. random vectors
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, a linear smoother estimator of
\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)
has the form of

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X=x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(x)Y_{i},

(12)

where
w_{i}(x)\in\mathbb{R}
are weights depending only on
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.

Lemma 3

(
Györfi et al., 2002
, Theorem 4.1)
.

Assume the following conditions are satisfied for any distribution of
X
:

(i)

There is a constant
c
such that for every non-negative measurable function
f
satisfying
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]<\infty
and any
n
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n}|w_{i}(X)|f(X_{i})\Bigg]\leq c\mathbb{E}[f(X)].

(ii)

There is a
D\geq 1
such that for all
n

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n}|w_{i}(X)|\leq D\Bigg]=1.

(iii)

For all
a>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n}|w_{i}(X)|\mathds{1}(\|X_{i}-X\|>a)\Bigg]=0.

(iv)

As
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(X)\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}1\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}(X)\Bigg]=0.

Then for all distributions of
(X,Y)
with
\mathbb{E}(Y^{2})<\infty
, the corresponding linear smoother
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X)
in (
12
) satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X)-\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)\big\}^{2}\bigr]=0.

The following (non-asymptotic Slutsky’s theorem) is well-known
(
Bentkus et al., 2009
, e.g.,)
. We provide a proof for completeness.

Lemma 4
.

For
T=L+\Delta
,
Z\sim N(0,1)
and
p>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(T\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(L\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|+2p^{\frac{1}{p+1}}\left(\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\!\right)^{\frac{p}{p+1}}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[|\Delta|^{p}]\bigr)^{\frac{1}{p+1}}.

Proof.

For any
\epsilon>0
, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(L+\Delta\leq t)=\mathbb{P}(L+\Delta\leq t,\ |\Delta|\leq\epsilon)+\mathbb{P}(L+\Delta\leq t,\ |\Delta|>\epsilon).

Thus the triangle inequality gives

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(T\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\max\biggl\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(L\leq x-\epsilon)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|,\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(L\leq x+\epsilon)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|\bigg\}+\mathbb{P}(|\Delta|^{p}>\epsilon^{p}).

By applying the triangle inequality again and using the Lipschitz property of
\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)
,

\displaystyle\max\biggl\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(L\leq x-\epsilon)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|,\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(L\leq x+\epsilon)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(L\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|+\epsilon\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\phi(x),

where
\phi
is the probability density function of
N(0,1)
. On the other hand, Markov’s inequality gives
\mathbb{P}(|\Delta|^{p}>\epsilon^{p})\leq\epsilon^{-p}\mathbb{E}[|\Delta|^{p}]
. Therefore

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(T\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\mathbb{P}(L\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(Z\leq x)\big|+\epsilon\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\phi(x)+\epsilon^{-p}\mathbb{E}[|\Delta|^{p}].

Optimizing the right-hand side over
\epsilon>0
yields the desired result.
∎

The following lemma due to
Esseen, 1942
presents a Berry–Esseen bound for non-identically distributed summands.

Lemma 5
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be independent random variables with
\mathbb{E}(X_{i})=0
,
\mathbb{E}(X_{i}^{2})=\sigma_{i}^{2}>0
and
\mathbb{E}(|X_{i}|^{3})=\rho_{i}<\infty
. Denote the standardized sum of
X_{i}
s as

\displaystyle S_{n}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{2}}}.

Then there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{P}(S_{n}\leq t)-\Phi(t)|\leq C\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{-3/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}\quad\text{for all $n$.}

Lemma 6

(
Yaskov, 2014
, Corollary 3.4)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be i.i.d. random vectors with
\mathbb{E}(X)=0
and
\mathrm{Var}(X)=\bm{I}_{d}
. Define
K_{d}=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|v\|_{2}=1}\mathbb{E}|X^{\top}v|
. Let
\widehat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}
. Then there are universal constants
C_{0},C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that with probability at least
1-\exp\{-C_{1}K_{d}^{4}n\}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\widehat{\Sigma})\geq C_{0}K_{d}^{2},

when
d/n\leq C_{2}K_{d}^{2}
.

Lemma 7
.

Consider
n
i.i.d. pairs
(X_{i},Y_{i})
drawn from
P_{XY}
and partition the support of
X
into
K
disjoint bins
B_{1},\ldots,B_{K}
. Let
X
be drawn from the marginal distribution
P_{X}
, independent of
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Then the absolute third moment of the histogram estimator
\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{f}(X)|^{3}]
where

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(x)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})Y_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{j}\in B_{k})}\mathds{1}(x\in B_{k})

is less than or equal to
\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{3}]
.

Proof.

Notice that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{f}(X)|^{3}\,|\,X\in B_{k}\bigr]~\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})|Y_{i}|^{3}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})}\biggm|X\in B_{k}\biggr]

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{=}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})\mathbb{E}\bigl[|Y_{i}|^{3}\,|\,X_{i}\in B_{k}\bigr]}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})}\biggm|X\in B_{k}\biggr]

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(iii)}}{=}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\mathbb{E}\bigl[|Y_{1}|^{3}\,|\,X_{1}\in B_{k}\bigr]\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})}\biggm|X\in B_{k}\biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\mathbb{E}\bigl[|Y_{1}|^{3}\,|\,X_{1}\in B_{k}\bigr]\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathds{1}(X_{i}\in B_{k})}\biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[|Y_{1}|^{3}\,|\,X_{1}\in B_{k}\bigr],

where step (i) uses Jensen’s inequality, step (ii) uses the law of total expectation, step (iii) holds since
\mathbb{E}[|Y_{1}|^{3}\,|\,X_{1}\in B_{k}]=\cdots=\mathbb{E}[|Y_{n}|^{3}\,|\,X_{n}\in B_{k}]
. Using this preliminary result together with the law of total expectation yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{f}(X)|^{3}\bigr]=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\widehat{f}(X)|^{3}\,|\,X\in B_{k}\bigr]\mathbb{P}(X\in B_{k})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}\bigl[|Y_{1}|^{3}\,|\,X_{1}\in B_{k}\bigr]\mathbb{P}(X_{1}\in B_{k})=\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{3}].

∎

The following lemma is useful in establishing the asymptotic equivalence in
Proposition

6
.

Lemma 8

(Chernoff Tail Bounds for Binomial)
.

Let
Z
follow a Binomial distribution with parameters
(n,p)
and denote
\mu=np
. Then for any
\rho\in(0,1)
,

•

Lower tail bound:
\mathbb{P}\{Z\leq(1-\rho)\mu\}\leq e^{-\frac{\mu\rho^{2}}{2}}
for any
\rho\in(0,1)
.

•

Upper tail bound:
\mathbb{P}\{Z\geq(1+\rho)\mu\}\leq e^{-\frac{\min\{\rho,\rho^{2}\}\mu}{4}}
for any
\rho\geq 0
.

Proof.

See, e.g.,
Mulzer, 2018
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of Main Results

This section collects the proofs of the results in the main text.

C.1
Proof of
Theorem

1

We start by proving the asymptotic Normality result, and then proceed to establish the convergence result in terms of the MSPE.

Claim 1: Asymptotic Normality.

Given a fixed function
f
, we denote the semi-supervised U-statistic using
f
as

\displaystyle U_{f}=U-\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})+\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}f(X_{i}).

In
Part 1
of this proof, we show under the conditions of
Theorem

1
that

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n}(U_{f}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1),

(13)

and then in
Part 2
we leverage this result to prove the claim for
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
.

Part 1. Asymptotic Normality of
U_{f}
.
Since
U_{f}
remains invariant to a location-shift of
f
, we will assume that
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]=\psi
without loss of generality. By the Hoeffding decomposition, the semi-supervised U-statistic
U_{f}
can be written as

\displaystyle U_{f}=\underbrace{\psi+\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\ell_{1}(Y_{i})-f(X_{i})\}+\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\psi\}}_{:=L_{f}}+R,

where the remainder term
R
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[R]=0
and
\mathrm{Var}[R]=O(n^{-2})
by
Lee, 1990
. Therefore, by Chebyshev’s inequality, we have the relationship
U_{f}=L_{f}+o_{P}(n^{-1/2})
. Given this asymptotic equivalence, once we prove

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n}(L_{f}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1)\quad\text{as $n\rightarrow\infty$,}

(14)

the first claim on asymptotic Normality follows by Slutsky’s theorem. We note that
L_{f}-\psi
can be written as the sum of independent random variables
L_{f}-\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}Z_{i}
where

\displaystyle Z_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{r}{n}\{\ell_{1}(Y_{i})-f(X_{i})\}+\frac{r}{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\psi\}&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{r}{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\psi\}&\text{for $n+1\leq i\leq n+m$.}\end{cases}

We remark that
Z_{i}
are not identically distributed, which makes the conventional central limit theorem for i.i.d. summands not applicable. Instead, we leverage Lindeberg’s central limit theorem for triangular arrays. Since we assume
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]=\psi
, it can be seen by the law of total expectation that each
Z_{i}
is centered at zero, and by letting
A:=\ell_{1}(Y)-f(X)
and
B:=f(X)-\psi

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(Z_{i})=\begin{cases}\frac{r^{2}}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}[A^{2}]+\frac{r^{2}}{(n+m)^{2}}\mathbb{E}[B^{2}]+\frac{2r^{2}}{n(n+m)}\mathbb{E}[AB]&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{r^{2}}{(n+m)^{2}}\mathbb{E}[B^{2}]&\text{for $n+1\leq i\leq n+m$.}\end{cases}

Defining

\displaystyle s^{2}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathrm{Var}(Z_{i})=\frac{r^{2}}{n}\mathbb{E}[A^{2}]+\frac{r^{2}}{n+m}\mathbb{E}[B^{2}]+\frac{2r^{2}}{n+m}\mathbb{E}[AB],

the asymptotic Normality (
14
) holds if Lindeberg’s condition is fulfilled, i.e., for any fixed
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{s^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z_{i}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{i}|>\epsilon s)\bigr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\biggl\{\frac{n}{s^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z_{1}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{1}|>\epsilon s)\bigr]+\frac{m}{s^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z_{n+1}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{n+1}|>\epsilon s)\bigr]\biggr\}=0.

First of all, the finite second moment condition for
\ell
and
f
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[n^{2}Z_{1}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{1}|>\epsilon s)]\leq\mathbb{E}[n^{2}Z_{1}^{2}]\leq r^{2}(\mathbb{E}[A^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[B^{2}]+2|\mathbb{E}[AB]|)<\infty,

and
n^{2}Z_{1}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{1}|>\epsilon s)
converges to zero almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
for any fixed
\epsilon
. Moreover, it can be seen that

\displaystyle ns^{2}=\Lambda_{n,m,f}\geq r^{2}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X\}]+\frac{r^{2}n}{n+m}\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X\}],

where the inequality holds by
Lemma

2
. Since we assume
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X\}]>0
, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}ns^{2}>0.

(15)

Therefore, the dominated convergence theorem ensures that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n}{s^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z_{1}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{1}|>\epsilon s)\bigr]=0.

A similar argument shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[mnZ_{n+1}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{n+1}|>\epsilon s)\bigr]\leq r^{2}\mathbb{E}[B^{2}]<\infty

and
mnZ_{n+1}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{n+1}|>\epsilon s)
converges to zero almost surely as
n\rightarrow\infty
for any fixed
\epsilon>0
. Hence, again, the dominated convergence theorem along with (
15
) shows that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{m}{s^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[Z_{n+1}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{n+1}|>\epsilon s)\bigr]=0.

Consequently, Lindeberg’s condition holds and this proves the claim (
13
).

Part 2. Asymptotic Normality of
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
.
Given the result (
13
), the asymptotic Normality of
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
follows by Slutsky’s theorem once we prove

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-U_{f})^{2}]=o(n^{-1}).

(16)

Let
n_{0}=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
and
m_{0}=\lfloor m/2\rfloor
. Then by the definition of
\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}
, the difference between
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
and
U_{f}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{cross}}-U_{f}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+m_{0}}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}}_{\mathrm{(I)}}

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n+m_{0}+1}^{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}}_{\mathrm{(II)}}.

As
\mathbb{E}[(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-U_{f})^{2}]\leq 2\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{I})^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{II})^{2}]
, and due to the symmetry between (I) and (II), it suffices to prove that
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{(I)}^{2}]=o(n^{-1})
. Writing
\mathbb{E}[f(X)-\widehat{f}_{1}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{1}]=\Delta_{\widehat{f}_{1}}
, we can express the term (I) as

\displaystyle\mathrm{(I)}~=~

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})-\Delta_{\widehat{f}_{1}}\}}_{:=\mathrm{(I)}_{1}}-\underbrace{\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})-\Delta_{\widehat{f}_{1}}\}}_{:=\mathrm{(I)}_{2}}

\displaystyle-\underbrace{\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+m_{0}}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})-\Delta_{\widehat{f}_{1}}\}}_{:=\mathrm{(I)}_{3}}+a_{n,m}\Delta_{\widehat{f}_{1}},

where

\displaystyle a_{n,m}=\frac{r(\lfloor n/2\rfloor m-n\lfloor m/2\rfloor)}{n(n+m)}.

Then by the elementary inequality:
(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4})^{2}\leq 4(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}+x_{4}^{2})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{(I)}^{2}]\leq 4\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{I})_{1}^{2}]+4\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{I})_{2}^{2}]+4\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{I})_{3}^{2}]+4\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{\widehat{f}_{1}}^{2}]a_{n,m}^{2},

and by the law of expectation, and the (conditional) independence between summands,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{I})_{1}^{2}]=\frac{r^{2}n_{0}}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{Var}\{f(X)-\widehat{f}_{1}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{1}\}\bigr]\leq\frac{r^{2}n_{0}}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\{f(X)-\widehat{f}_{1}(X)\}^{2}\bigr]=o(n^{-1}).

Similarly, the other terms satisfy that
\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{I})_{2}^{2}]=o(n^{-1})
and
\mathbb{E}[(\mathrm{I})_{3}^{2}]=o(n^{-1})
. Lastly, it holds that
a_{n,m}^{2}=O(n^{-2})
for any integers
n,m
, and so
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{(I)}^{2}]=o(n^{-1})
, which again proves the claim (
16
).

Claim 2: Convergence of MSE.

For the second claim, we write
U_{f}-\psi:=H_{f}+R
where

\displaystyle H_{f}=\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\ell_{1}(Y_{i})-f(X_{i})\}+\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\psi\}.

Noting that
\mathbb{E}[H_{f}]=0
and
\mathrm{Var}[H_{f}]=n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}
, we have the identity that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(U_{f}-\psi\bigr)^{2}\bigr]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}=1+\frac{2\mathbb{E}[H_{f}R]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}+\frac{\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}.

(17)

As mentioned earlier,
R
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[R]=0
and
\mathrm{Var}[R]=O(n^{-2})
by
Lee, 1990
. Hence the last term in the above display converges to zero. Similarly the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality yields that the second term fulfills

\displaystyle\frac{2|\mathbb{E}[H_{f}R]|}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{E}[H_{f}^{2}]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}=2\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}=o(1).

As a result, the ratio (
17
) converges to one as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Furthermore, given the following decomposition:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-\psi\bigr)^{2}\bigr]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-U_{f}+U_{f}-\psi\bigr)^{2}\bigr]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(U_{f}-U_{\mathrm{cross}}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(U_{f}-\psi\bigr)^{2}\bigr]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}+\frac{2\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-U_{f}\bigr)\bigl(U_{f}-\psi\bigr)\bigr]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}},

the second claim in
Theorem

1
follows once we show

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(U_{f}-U_{\mathrm{cross}}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]}{n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}}=o(1).

Remark that we already proved in (
16
) that
\mathbb{E}[(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-U_{f})^{2}]=o(n^{-1})
, and
\Lambda_{n,m,f}\geq r^{2}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X\}]>0
. Therefore the above claim follows, and the third term in the decomposition is also
o(1)
, which can be verified by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. This completes the proof of
Theorem

1
.

C.2
Proof of
Lemma

2

Note that minimizing
\Lambda_{n,m,f}
is equivalent to minimizing

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[f(X)]-2\mathrm{Cov}[f(X),\psi_{1}(X)]=\mathrm{Var}[\psi_{1}(X)-f(X)]-\mathrm{Var}[\psi_{1}(X)].

As the variance is non-negative, this expression is lower bounded by
-\mathrm{Var}[\psi_{1}(X)]
, which can be achieved when
\psi_{1}=f
. Hence the result follows.

C.3
Proof of
Proposition

1

To prove the claim, we upper bound the MSPE using condition (i) and applying the inequality
(x+y)^{2}\leq 2x^{2}+2y^{2}
twice as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]-\mathbb{E}[\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]\}^{2}]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)-\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]+\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]+R-\mathbb{E}[\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)-\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]\}^{2}]+4\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]-\mathbb{E}[\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]\}^{2}]+4\mathbb{E}[R^{2}].

The upper bound is
o(1)
under conditions (i), (ii) and (iii), which completes the proof of
Proposition

1
.

C.4
Proof of
Proposition

2

For simplicity, assume that
n
is even. For the linear smoother, condition (ii) holds with
R=0
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)\,|\,X=x]~=~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}w_{i}(x)\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}w_{i}(x)\big\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\big\}+\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}w_{i}(x)\ell_{1}(Y_{i})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)-\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X=x]+\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X=x].

For condition (iii), writing the rescaled weight function as

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}_{i}(X)=\frac{w_{i}(X)}{\sum_{j=n/4+1}^{n/2}w_{j}(X)},

we can observe a series of inequalities:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)-\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]\}^{2}]~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bigg(\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}w_{i}(X)\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}\bigg)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\lesssim}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bigg(\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}\widetilde{w}_{i}(X)\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}\bigg)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{\lesssim}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}\widetilde{w}_{i}(X)\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\biggr],

where step (i) uses the condition
\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}w_{i}(x)\leq C
and step (ii) holds by Jensen’s inequality. By the law of total expectation and independence from sample splitting, the last expectation can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}\widetilde{w}_{i}(X)\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\biggr]~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=n/4+1}^{n/2}\widetilde{w}_{i}(X)\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\,|\,X_{i}\Bigr)\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\max_{n/4+1\leq i\leq n/2}\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\,|\,X_{i}\Bigr)\biggr].

Observe that
\widehat{\ell}_{1}(y)
is a U-statistic of
\ell_{1}(y)
with the variance bounded above as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(y)-\ell_{1}(y)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]\lesssim\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\ell^{2}(y,Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r-1})]:=\frac{g(y)}{n},

(18)

which follows by
Lee, 1990
. Moreover noting that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[|\mathbb{E}[g(Y)\,|\,X]|\bigr]\leq\mathbb{E}[g(Y)]=\mathbb{E}[\ell^{2}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})]<\infty,

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\max_{n/4+1\leq i\leq n/2}\mathbb{E}\Bigl(\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\,|\,X_{i}\Bigr)\biggr]\lesssim n^{-1}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\max_{n/4+1\leq i\leq n/2}\mathbb{E}[g(Y_{i})\,|\,X_{i}]\Bigr]=o(1),

where the last equality makes use of the following result that if
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
are i.i.d. random variable with
\mathbb{E}[|Z_{1}|]<\infty
, then
\mathbb{E}[\max_{1\leq i\leq n}Z_{i}]=o(n)

(
Downey, 1990
)
. This implies
\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{\mathbb{E}}[\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)-\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X]\}^{2}]=o(1)
as desired.

C.5
Proof of
Theorem

2

Let us denote as
U_{f}
the semi-supervised U-statistic using the target assistant-function
f
. In view of the proof of
Theorem

1
, it suffices to prove

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{plug}}-U_{f}=o_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

This condition is met under (i) Donsker condition in
Theorem

2
, followed by
van der Vaart, 2000
. Therefore, we focus on (ii) stability condition in
Theorem

2
and prove the above asymptotic equivalence between
U_{\mathrm{plug}}
and
U_{f}
.

Letting
\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}
be the expectation with respect to
X_{i}
conditional on everything else, we write
U_{\mathrm{plug}}-U_{f}
as

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{plug}}-U_{f}~=~

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}(X_{i})\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I})+(\mathrm{II})+(\mathrm{III}),

where

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I}):=\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})+\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]-\mathbb{E}[f(X)]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 25.00003pt+\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}(X_{i})\}+\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}(X_{i})]-\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]\},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{II}):=-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})+\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]-\mathbb{E}[f(X)]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 25.00003pt-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}(X_{i})]-\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]\},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{III}):=-\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]+\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]

\displaystyle\hskip 25.00003pt+\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})].

Le us start by analyzing the first term
(\mathrm{I}):=(\mathrm{I})_{a}+(\mathrm{I})_{b}+(\mathrm{I})_{c}
where

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I})_{a}:=\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})+\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]-\mathbb{E}[f(X)]\},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I})_{b}:=\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}(X_{i})\},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I})_{c}:=\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}(X_{i})]-\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]\},

and we see that both
(\mathrm{I})_{b}
and
(\mathrm{I})_{c}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|(\mathrm{I})_{b}|]\leq r\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big|\widehat{f}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})\big|\bigr]\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|(\mathrm{I})_{c}|]\leq r\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big|\widehat{f}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})\big|\bigr].

For the term
(\mathrm{I})_{a}
, letting
W_{i}:=f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})+\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]-\mathbb{E}[f(X)]
, we have

\displaystyle n\mathbb{E}[\{(\mathrm{I})_{a}\}^{2}]=\frac{r^{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[W_{i}^{2}]+\frac{r^{2}}{n}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\mathbb{E}[W_{i}W_{j}].

Since
X_{i}
is independent of
\widehat{f}^{(-i)}
, we observe that
\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]=\mathbb{E}_{X}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)]
, which leads to

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[W_{i}^{2}]\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}(X)-f(X)\}^{2}]=o(1).

Next, for
i\neq j
, we build on the proof idea of double centering in
Chen et al., 2022
. In particular, define
W_{i}^{(-j)}
and
W_{j}^{(-i)}
similarly as
W_{i}
and
W_{j}
, respectively, by replacing
(X_{j},Y_{j})
in
W_{i}
and
(X_{i},Y_{i})
in
W_{j}
with their i.i.d. copies. Then we have
\mathbb{E}_{X_{j}}[W_{j}W_{i}^{(-j)}]=0
and thus the law of total expectation yields
\mathbb{E}[W_{j}W_{i}^{(-j)}]=0
. Similarly, we have
\mathbb{E}[W_{i}W_{j}^{(-i)}]=0
. This along with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality leads to

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}[W_{i}W_{j}]|~=~

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\bigl[(W_{i}-W_{i}^{(-j)})(W_{j}-W_{j}^{(-i)})\bigr]\big|

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\big\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big(W_{i}-W_{i}^{(-j)}\big)^{2}\bigr]\big\}^{1/2}\big\{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big(W_{j}-W_{j}^{(-i)}\big)^{2}\bigr]\big\}^{1/2}.

We let
\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}
denote an estimate of
f
trained on
\mathcal{D}_{XY}^{(-i,-j)}
, that is the same as
\mathcal{D}_{XY}
except
(X_{i},Y_{i})
and
(X_{j},Y_{j})
replaced by their i.i.d. copies. Then using the inequality
(x+y)^{2}\leq 2x^{2}+2y^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big(W_{i}-W_{i}^{(-j)}\big)^{2}\bigr]\leq 2\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}(X_{i})\}^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}(X)\}^{2}]\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big(W_{j}-W_{j}^{(-i)}\big)\bigr]\leq 2\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}^{(-j)}(X_{j})-\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}(X_{j})\}^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}^{(-j)}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}(X)\}^{2}].

Moreover, since
X_{i}
is not used in the construction of both
\widehat{f}^{(-i)}
and
\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}
, the following identity holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}(X_{i})|^{2}]~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}(X)|^{2}]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{f}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-j)}(X)|^{2}],

where the second equality follows since
\widehat{f}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-j)}(X)
and
\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-i,-j)}(X)
have the same distribution. This leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big(W_{i}-W_{i}^{(-j)}\big)^{2}\bigr]\leq 4\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)\}^{2}].

Putting things together, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{r^{2}}{n}\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}\mathbb{E}[W_{i}W_{j}]\bigg|~\lesssim~n\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)\}^{2}]=o(1)

and thus conclude that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}[|(\mathrm{I})|]=o(1).

The second term (II) can be analyzed analogously as (I) and shown to be
\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}[|(\mathrm{II})|]=o(1)
.

For the last term (III), as we assume
\widehat{f}
is trained on the entire labeled dataset
\mathcal{D}_{XY}
,
\widehat{f}^{(-i)}
remains the same as
\widehat{f}
for
n+1\leq i\leq n+m
. Thus the last two sums in the term (III) satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\frac{rm}{n(n+m)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]-\frac{rm}{n+m}\mathbb{E}_{X}[\widehat{f}(X)]

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\frac{rm}{n+m}\times\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]-\mathbb{E}_{X}[\widehat{f}(X)]\big\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\frac{rm}{n+m}\times\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathbb{E}_{X}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)-\widehat{f}(X)]\big\},

where the last equality utilizes the observation that
\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})]=\mathbb{E}_{X}[\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)]
as
\widehat{f}^{(-i)}
is independent of
X_{i}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}[|(\mathrm{III})|]\lesssim\sqrt{n}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big|\widehat{f}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X_{i})\big|\bigr]+\sqrt{n}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big|\widehat{f}(X)-\widehat{f}^{(-i)}(X)\big|\bigr]=o(1).

We have shown that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|U_{\mathrm{plug}}-U_{f}|]=o(n^{-1/2}),

which together with Markov’s inequality proves the desired claim
U_{\mathrm{plug}}-U_{f}=o_{P}(n^{-1/2})
.

C.6
Proof of
Theorem

3

The proof of
Theorem

3
builds on the Berry–Esseen bound for non-linear statistics
(
Chen et al., 2011
, Chapter 10)
and
Lemma

4
. For notational simplicity, let us write

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}:=n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}=\frac{r^{2}}{n}\biggl(\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\}+\frac{m}{n+m}\{\mathrm{Var}[f(X)]-2\mathrm{Cov}[f(X),\psi_{1}(X)]\}\biggr)\geq\frac{r^{2}\sigma_{1}^{2}}{n},

(19)

where the inequality follows by
Lemma

2
, which shows that
\psi_{1}
minimizes
\Lambda_{n,m,f}
as a function of
f
, and
\Lambda_{n,m,f}
defined with
\psi_{1}
is greater than or equal to
r^{2}\sigma_{1}^{2}/n
. Using
Chen et al., 2011
, we observe that
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\frac{U_{\mathrm{cross}}-\psi}{\sigma}~=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{r}{\sigma n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\ell_{1}(Y_{i})-f(X_{i})\}+\frac{r}{\sigma(n+m)}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\psi\}}_{=W}

(20)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sigma}\binom{n}{r}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leq n}\biggl(\ell(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{r}})-\psi-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\{\ell_{1}(Y_{i_{j}})-\psi\}\biggr)}_{=\Delta_{1}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{r}{\sigma n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})\}+\frac{r}{\sigma(n+m)}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-f(X_{i})\}}_{=\Delta_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W+\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}.

As in the proof of
Theorem

1
, we note that
W
can be written as the sum of independent random variables
Z_{i}
, i.e.,
W=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}Z_{i}
, where

\displaystyle Z_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{r}{\sigma n}\{\ell_{1}(Y_{i})-f(X_{i})\}+\frac{r}{\sigma(n+m)}\{f(X_{i})-\psi\}&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{r}{\sigma(n+m)}\{f(X_{i})-\psi\}&\text{for $n+1\leq i\leq n+m$.}\end{cases}

For
k\in[n]
, let
\Delta_{1,k}
denote a leave-one-out version of
\Delta_{1}
, excluding
Y_{k}
in its calculation, defined as

\displaystyle\Delta_{1,k}=\frac{1}{\sigma}\binom{n}{r}^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leq n\\
\text{$i_{q}\neq k$ for all $q$}\end{subarray}}\biggl(\ell(Y_{i_{1}},\ldots,Y_{i_{r}})-\psi-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\{\ell_{1}(Y_{i_{j}})-\psi\}\biggr),

and set
\Delta_{1,k}=\Delta_{1}
for
k\in[n+m]\setminus[n]
. For
k\in[n]
, we let
\Delta_{2,k}
be similarly computed as
\Delta_{2}
but by replacing
(X_{k},Y_{k})
with its i.i.d. copy
(\widetilde{X}_{k},\widetilde{Y}_{k})
. For
k\in[n+m]\!\setminus\![n]
, we let
\Delta_{2,k}
be similarly computed as
\Delta_{2}
but by replacing
X_{k}
with its i.i.d. copy
\widetilde{X}_{k}
. This construction ensures that
Z_{i}
, which is a function of
(X_{i},Y_{i})
or
X_{i}
only, is independent of
(W-Z_{i},\Delta_{1,i}+\Delta_{2,i})
for each
i\in[n+m]
.

Letting
\Delta=\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}
,
Chen et al., 2011
yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}\leq t\biggr)-\Phi(t)\bigg|~\leq~

\displaystyle 6.1(\beta_{2}+\beta_{3})+\mathbb{E}[|W\Delta|]+\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}|Z_{i}(\Delta-\Delta_{1,i}-\Delta_{2,i})|

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 6.1(\mathrm{I})+\mathrm{(II)}+\mathrm{(III)},

where

\displaystyle\beta_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}\mathds{1}(|Z_{i}|>1)]\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{3}=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}[|Z_{i}|^{3}\mathds{1}(|Z_{i}|\leq 1)].

We next provide upper bounds for (I), (II) and (III) in order.

Analysis of (I).

Starting with
\mathrm{(I)}=\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}
, recall

\displaystyle M_{p,\ell_{1}}=\mathbb{E}[|\ell_{1}(Y)-\mathbb{E}[\ell_{1}(Y)]|^{p}]\quad\text{and}\quad M_{p,f}=\mathbb{E}[|f(X)-\mathbb{E}[f(X)]|^{p}].

Then using the inequality
\sigma^{3}\gtrsim\sigma_{1}^{3}n^{-3/2}
from (
19
), we have

\displaystyle\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}[|Z_{i}|^{3}]=2n\mathbb{E}[|Z_{1}|^{3}]+2m\mathbb{E}[|Z_{n+1}|^{3}]~\lesssim~\frac{M_{3,\ell_{1}}+M_{3,f}}{\sqrt{n}\sigma_{1}^{3}},

which yields the first term in
\Omega_{1}
in the theorem statement.

Analysis of (II).

For the second term
(\mathrm{II})=\mathbb{E}[|W\Delta|]
, the triangle inequality along with Jensen’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|W\Delta|]\leq\mathbb{E}[|W\Delta_{1}|]+\mathbb{E}[|W\Delta_{2}|]\leq\{\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{1}^{2}]\}^{1/2}+\{\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{2}^{2}]\}^{1/2},

where we use the condition
\mathbb{E}[W^{2}]=1
or equivalently
\mathrm{Var}[\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}Z_{i}]=\sigma^{2}
. Moreover,
Chen et al., 2011
yields

\displaystyle\{\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{1}^{2}]\}^{1/2}\leq\bigg\{\frac{(r-1)^{2}\mathrm{Var}[\ell(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})]}{r(n-r+1)\mathrm{Var}[\ell_{1}(Y)]}\biggr\}^{1/2}\lesssim\frac{\sigma_{\ell}}{(n-r)\sigma_{1}},

where we use the inequality
\mathrm{Var}[\ell_{1}(Y)]\geq\sigma_{1}^{2}
and recall that
\sigma_{\ell}^{2}=\mathrm{Var}[\ell(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})]
. The next term
\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{2}^{2}]
is analyzed in the proof of
Theorem

1
, and it can be shown that

\displaystyle\{\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{2}^{2}]\}^{1/2}\lesssim\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{MSPE}}^{1/2}}{\sigma_{1}}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|W\Delta|]\lesssim\frac{\sigma_{\ell}}{\sqrt{n-r}\sigma_{1}}+\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{MSPE}}^{1/2}}{\sigma_{1}}.

Analysis of (III).

For the last term
\mathrm{(III)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}|Z_{i}(\Delta-\Delta_{1,i}-\Delta_{2,i})|
, we first apply the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}|Z_{i}(\Delta-\Delta_{1,i}-\Delta_{2,i})|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}|Z_{i}(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{1,i})|+\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}|Z_{i}(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{2,i})|.

Note that
\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{1,i}=0
for
i\in[n+m]\setminus[n]
. So using
Chen et al., 2011
along with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality yields

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}|Z_{i}(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{1,i})|\ =\

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}|Z_{i}(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{1,i})|

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle n\{\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}^{2}]\}^{1/2}\{\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{1,1})^{2}]\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\

\displaystyle\frac{(M_{2,\ell_{1}}^{1/2}+M_{2,f}^{1/2})}{\sigma_{1}}\times\biggr[\frac{2(r-1)\mathrm{Var}[\ell(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})]}{nr(n-r+1)\sigma_{1}^{2}}\biggr]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\

\displaystyle\frac{(M_{2,\ell_{1}}^{1/2}+M_{2,f}^{1/2})\sigma_{\ell}}{\sqrt{n-r}\sigma_{1}^{2}}.

Turning to the next term, we use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to yield

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}|Z_{i}(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{2,i})|\ \leq\

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]\}^{1/2}\{\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{2,i})^{2}]\}^{1/2},

where the second moment of
Z_{i}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{i}^{2}]\lesssim\begin{cases}\frac{1}{n\sigma_{1}^{2}}\big\{\mathrm{Var}[\ell_{1}(Y)]+\mathrm{Var}[f(X)]\big\},\quad&\text{if $i\in[n]$,}\\[5.0pt]
\frac{1}{n\sigma_{1}^{2}}\frac{n^{2}}{(n+m)^{2}}\mathrm{Var}[f(X)],\quad&\text{if $i\in[n+m]\setminus[n]$.}\end{cases}

To deal with
\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{2,i})^{2}]
, we let
n_{0}=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
and
m_{0}=\lfloor m/2\rfloor
, and denote by
\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}
the estimator similarly defined as
\widehat{f}_{2}
but replacing
(X_{1},Y_{1})
with i.i.d. copy
(\widetilde{X}_{1},\widetilde{Y}_{1})
. Then by first fixing
i=1
, consider the following decomposition as in the proof of
Theorem

1
:

\displaystyle\sigma\times(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{2,1})

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n}\{f(X_{1})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{1})-f(\widetilde{X}_{1})+\widehat{f}_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\{f(X_{1})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{1})-f(\widetilde{X}_{1})+\widehat{f}_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\}

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}

\displaystyle-~

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n+m_{0}+1}^{n+m}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{rm}{n(n+m)}\{f(X_{1})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{1})-f(\widetilde{X}_{1})+\widehat{f}_{1}(\widetilde{X}_{1})\}+\frac{rm}{n(n+m)}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}

\displaystyle-~

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n+m_{0}+1}^{n+m}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}.

Moreover observe that

\displaystyle\frac{rm}{n(n+m)}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}-\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n+m_{0}+1}^{n+m}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{rm}{n(n+m)}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}]-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})+\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}]\}

\displaystyle-~

\displaystyle\frac{r}{n+m}\sum_{i=n+m_{0}+1}^{n+m}\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}]-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})+\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}]\}

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{r}{n+m}\times\frac{m\lfloor n/2\rfloor-n\lfloor m/2\rfloor}{n}}_{\asymp~n^{-1}}\times\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}]-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}]\bigr),

where the summands in the alternative expression are centered. Using this observation, it can be seen that

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{2,1})^{2}]

\displaystyle~\lesssim~

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}}{n^{2}(n+m)^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}_{1}(X)-f(X)\}^{2}]+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X)-\widehat{f}_{2}(X)\}^{2}]

\displaystyle~~+\frac{m^{2}}{n^{2}(n+m)^{2}}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}]-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})+\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}]\}^{2}]

\displaystyle~~+\frac{1}{(n+m)^{2}}\sum_{i=n+m_{0}+1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}[\{\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}^{-(1)}]-\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{i})+\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{2}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}]\}^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim~
