The following integrals are finite

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{\{g^{\prime}(t)\}^{2}}{g(t)}dt<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{\{g^{\prime\prime}(t)\}^{2}}{g(t)}dt<\infty.

One possible candidate for such
g
can be constructed as follows. Without loss of generality, let
t_{0}=-1
and
t_{1}=1
, and define

\displaystyle g(t)=C_{g}\cdot e^{-t^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{1-t^{2}}}\mathds{1}(|t|\leq 1),

where
C_{g}\approx 0.384
is the normalizing constant. It can be checked that the above
g
satisfies all of the previous conditions with
t_{0}=-1
and
t_{1}=1
. To consider a general support, let us write

\displaystyle J_{1}:=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\bigl\{g^{\prime}(t)\bigr\}^{2}}{g(t)}dt<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad J_{2}:=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\bigl\{g^{\prime\prime}(t)\bigr\}^{2}}{g(t)}dt<\infty.

Then a transformed variable
t_{a,b}=a+bt
has the density function

\displaystyle g_{a,b}(t)=\frac{1}{b}g\left(\frac{t-a}{b}\right)

(29)

supported on
[a-b,a+b]
, and its density function fulfills

\displaystyle\int_{a-b}^{a+b}\frac{\bigl\{g_{a,b}^{\prime}(t)\bigr\}^{2}}{g_{a,b}(t)}dt=\frac{J_{1}}{b^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{a-b}^{a+b}\frac{\bigl\{g_{a,b}^{\prime\prime}(t)\bigr\}^{2}}{g_{a,b}(t)}dt=\frac{J_{2}}{b^{4}}.

We will use
g_{a,b}
as the prior density for
c
and
\delta
with the specific values of
a
and
b
to be determined later.

Main proof via the 1st/2nd-order van Trees Inequality.

As demonstrated earlier, the main idea of the van Trees inequality, again, is the use of integration by parts. Letting
\widehat{\psi}
be an arbitrary estimator of
\mu^{2}
and
g_{a,b,2}(\cdot,\cdot)=g_{a,b}(\cdot)g_{a,b}(\cdot)
, integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\int_{a-b}^{a+b}\int_{a-b}^{a+b}\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-(c+\delta)^{2}\bigr)\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\Biggl[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)\Biggr]d\delta dc

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int_{a-b}^{a+b}\int_{a-b}^{a+b}2(c+\delta)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b,2}(\delta,c)d\delta dc.

Therefore by integrating the above equations over
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{X_{i}\}_{i=n+1}^{n+m}
, and letting
\delta,c
be i.i.d. random variable with the density
g_{a,b}
in (
29
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,Y,c,\delta}\left[\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-(c+\delta)\bigr)^{2}\underbrace{\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)\right]}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)}}_{:=W_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}_{c,\delta}[c+\delta]=2\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mu],

where
\mathbb{E}_{X,Y,c,\delta}
denotes the expectation taken over
\{\mathcal{D}_{X,Y}
,
\mathcal{D}_{X},c,\delta\}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{c,\delta}
denotes the expectation taken over
\{c,\delta\}
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,Y,c,\delta}\left[\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-(c+\delta)\bigr)^{2}\underbrace{\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)\right]}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)}}_{:=W_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}_{c,\delta}[c+\delta]=2\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mu].

Next we define

\displaystyle V_{1}:=\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)\right]}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)},

\displaystyle V_{2}:=\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\delta^{2}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)\right]}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle V_{3}:=\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\delta\partial c}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)\right]}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta)g_{a,b}(\delta,c)}.

Under the conditions for
g_{a,b}
, another application of integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,Y,c,\delta}\Bigl[\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-(c+\delta)^{2}\bigr)V_{i}\Bigr]=-2\quad\text{for $i=1,2,3$.}

Hence for any
\bm{u}:=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4},u_{5})^{\top}\in\mathbb{S}^{4}:=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{5}:\|\bm{x}\|_{2}=1\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,Y,c,\delta}\Bigl[\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-(c+\delta)^{2}\bigr)\bigl(u_{1}W_{1}+u_{2}W_{2}+u_{3}V_{1}+u_{4}V_{2}+u_{5}V_{3}\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mu](u_{1}+u_{2})-2(u_{3}+u_{4}+u_{5}).

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, it can be seen that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,Y,c,\delta}\Bigl[\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-(c+\delta)^{2}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr]\geq\sup_{\bm{u}\in\mathbb{S}^{4}}\frac{(\bm{u}^{\top}\bm{\tau})^{2}}{\bm{u}^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}\bm{\eta}^{\top}]\bm{u}}=\bm{\tau}^{\top}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}\bm{\eta}^{\top}]\bigr)^{-1}\bm{\tau},

(30)

where
\bm{\tau}=(2\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mu],2\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mu],-2,-2,-2)^{\top}
and
\bm{\eta}=(W_{1},W_{2},V_{1},V_{2},V_{3})^{\top}
.

Now take
a=\mu_{0,n}/2
and
b=K/(2\sqrt{n})
where
\mu_{0,n}
is a sequence of real numbers in the theorem statement, and
K
is a constant. This choice makes
(\delta,c)
be supported on
[\frac{\mu_{0,n}}{2}\pm\frac{K}{2\sqrt{n}}]\times[\frac{\mu_{0,n}}{2}\pm\frac{K}{2\sqrt{n}}]
; therefore
\delta+c\in[\mu_{0,n}\pm\frac{K}{\sqrt{n}}]
. This leads to
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\mu]=\mu_{0,n}
since the distribution of
\delta+c
is symmetric around
\mu_{0,n}
by construction. Let
\rho
be the correlation between
X
and
Y
, i.e.,
\rho=\mathrm{Cov}(X,Y)/\{\mathrm{Var}(X)\mathrm{Var}(Y)\}^{1/2}
. Now as we shall show in what follows,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}\bm{\eta}^{\top}]
is a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{1}^{2}]=\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{2}^{2}]=\frac{n+m}{\sigma_{X}^{2}}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{1}^{2}]=\frac{2n^{2}}{(1-\rho^{2})^{2}(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{1}}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})K^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{2}}{K^{4}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{2}^{2}]=\frac{2(n+m)^{2}}{\sigma_{X}^{4}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{1}}{\sigma_{X}^{2}K^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{2}}{K^{4}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{3}^{2}]=\biggl\{\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}\biggr\}\times\biggl\{\frac{n+m}{\sigma_{X}^{2}}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}\biggr\}.

Therefore, the lower bound in (
30
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X,Y,c,\delta}\Bigl[\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-(c+\delta)^{2}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr]\geq\frac{4\mu_{0,n}^{2}}{\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}}+\frac{4\mu_{0,n}^{2}}{\frac{n+m}{\sigma_{X}^{2}}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}}+

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{\frac{2n^{2}}{(1-\rho^{2})^{2}(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{1}}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})K^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{2}}{K^{4}}}+\frac{4}{\frac{2(n+m)^{2}}{\sigma_{X}^{4}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{1}}{\sigma_{X}^{2}K^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{2}}{K^{4}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{\left(\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}\right)\times\left(\frac{n+m}{\sigma_{X}^{2}}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}\right)},

which implies that for a given sequence
\{\mu_{0,n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\liminf_{K\rightarrow\infty}\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{\widehat{\psi}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{mean}}:\\
|\mu_{P}-\mu_{0,n}|\leq\frac{K}{\sqrt{n}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-\mu_{P}^{2}\bigr)^{2}\bigr]}{4n^{-1}\mu_{0,n}^{2}\sigma_{m,n}^{2}+2n^{-2}\sigma_{m,n}^{4}}\geq 1.

where we recall

\displaystyle\sigma_{m,n}^{2}~=~

\displaystyle\underbrace{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}_{=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{n}{n+m}\sigma_{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X)]+\frac{n}{n+m}\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)].

Calculation of
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}\bm{\eta}^{\top}]
.

It remains to prove that the matrix
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}\bm{\eta}^{\top}]
is a diagonal matrix with the diagonal entries specified earlier. To simplify the notation, let us denote

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&f_{1}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i}),\quad f_{2}=\prod_{j=1}^{n+m}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta),\\[5.0pt]
&g_{a,b,2}(\delta,c)=g_{a,b}(\delta)g_{a,b}(c),\quad g_{c}=g_{a,b}(c),\quad g_{\delta}=g_{a,b}(\delta),\\[5.0pt]
&f_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{\partial}{\partial c}f_{1},\quad f_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}f_{1},\quad f_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}f_{2},\quad f_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\delta^{2}}f_{2},\\[5.0pt]
&g_{c}^{\prime}=\frac{\partial}{\partial c}g_{1},\quad g_{c}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}g_{c},\quad g_{\delta}^{\prime}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}g_{\delta},\quad g_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\delta^{2}}g_{\delta},\end{cases}

and write

\displaystyle W_{1}=\frac{f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}},\ W_{2}=\frac{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}},

\displaystyle V_{1}=\frac{f_{1}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}+2f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}},\ V_{2}=\frac{f_{2}^{\prime\prime}g_{\delta}+2f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}^{\prime}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle V_{3}=\frac{(f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime})}{f_{1}g_{c}}\times\frac{(f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime})}{f_{2}g_{\delta}},

which holds by the product rule. The expectation of
W_{1}^{2}
is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{1}^{2}]~=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{(f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime})^{2}}{f_{1}^{2}g_{c}^{2}}f_{1}f_{2}g_{c}g_{\delta}d\nu=\int\frac{f_{1}^{{}^{\prime}2}g_{c}^{2}+f_{1}^{2}g_{c}^{{}^{\prime}2}+2f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}}f_{2}g_{\delta}d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{(f^{\prime}_{1})^{2}}{f_{1}}d\nu+\int\frac{(g_{c}^{\prime})^{2}}{g_{c}}d\nu=\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}

and the expectation of
W_{2}^{2}
can be similarly computed as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{2}^{2}]=\frac{n+m}{\sigma_{X}^{2}}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}.

Before computing the expectations including
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
, observe that the product rule yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\Biggl[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\Biggr]~=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\Biggl[\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\log\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\Biggr]

(31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\log\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\log\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)^{2}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i}),

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\Biggl[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})g_{a,b}(c)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\Biggl[\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)g_{a,b}(c)+\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\frac{\partial}{\partial c}g_{a,b}(c)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)g_{a,b}(c)+2\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial c}g_{a,b}(c)\biggr)

\displaystyle+\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}g_{a,b}(c).

Therefore we can write

\displaystyle\Biggl[\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\big\{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})g_{a,b}(c)\big\}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})g_{a,b}(c)}\Biggr]^{2}=A^{2}+B^{2}+C^{2}+2AB+2AC+2BC,

where

\displaystyle A=\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})},\ B=\frac{2\bigl(\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\bigr)\bigl(\frac{\partial}{\partial c}g_{a,b}(c)\bigr)}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})g_{a,b}(c)}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle C=\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}g_{a,b}(c)}{g_{a,b}(c)}.

Using the expression (
31
), we can compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[A^{2}]~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\Biggl\{\biggl(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\log\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)+\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\log\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)^{2}\Biggr\}^{2}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl\{-\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\biggl(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-X_{i}-c)}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\biggr)^{2}\bigg\}^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-X_{i}-c)}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]=\frac{2n^{2}}{(1-\rho^{2})^{2}(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{2}}

based on the observations that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\log\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})=-\frac{1}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\log\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-X_{i}-c)}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}.

Similar calculations show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[B^{2}]=\frac{4nJ_{1}}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})b^{2}},\ \mathbb{E}[C^{2}]=\frac{J_{2}}{b^{4}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}[AC]=\mathbb{E}[BC]=0

Therefore, letting
b=K/(2\sqrt{n})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{1}^{2}]=\frac{2n^{2}}{(1-\rho^{2})^{2}(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{1}}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})K^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{2}}{K^{4}}.

By symmetry,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{2}^{2}]=\frac{2(n+m)^{2}}{\sigma_{X}^{4}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{1}}{\sigma_{X}^{2}K^{2}}+\frac{16n^{2}J_{2}}{K^{4}}.

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{3}^{2}]~=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl\{\frac{(f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime})}{f_{1}g_{c}}\biggr\}^{2}\biggr]\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl\{\frac{(f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime})}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}\biggr\}^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\biggl\{\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}\biggr\}\times\biggl\{\frac{n+m}{\sigma_{X}^{2}}+\frac{4nJ_{1}}{K^{2}}\biggr\}.

We next argue that
\mathbb{E}[V_{1}V_{2}]=\mathbb{E}[V_{1}V_{3}]=\mathbb{E}[V_{2}V_{3}]=0
. To start with
V_{1}V_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{1}V_{2}]=\int\{f_{1}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}+2f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime\prime}\}\{f_{2}^{\prime\prime}g_{\delta}+2f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}^{\prime}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}\}d\nu=0,

which can be shown using the observations that

\displaystyle\int f_{1}^{\prime}d\nu=\int\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\log\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr\}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})d\nu=0

and

\displaystyle\int f_{1}^{\prime\prime}d\nu=

\displaystyle\int\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c^{2}}\log\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})+\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\log\phi_{Y\,|\,X}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})\biggr)^{2}\bigg\}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,c,X_{i})d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle 0.

Similarly, it can be shown that

\displaystyle\int f_{2}^{\prime}d\nu=\int f_{2}^{\prime\prime}d\nu=0\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\int g_{c}^{\prime}d\nu=\int g_{c}^{\prime\prime}d\nu=\int g_{\delta}^{\prime}d\nu=\int g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}d\nu=0.

These ingredients yield that
\mathbb{E}[V_{1}V_{2}]=0
.

For the term
V_{1}V_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{1}V_{3}]=\int\frac{f_{1}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}+2f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}}\times\{f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}\}\times\{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}\}d\nu=0,

which can be verified using the following results:

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{1}^{\prime\prime}f_{1}^{\prime}}{f_{1}}d\nu=\int f_{1}^{\prime}d\nu=\int f_{1}^{\prime\prime}d\nu=\int f_{2}^{\prime}d\nu=\int g_{c}^{\prime}d\nu=\int g_{\delta}^{\prime}d\nu=0,

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{1}^{\prime}f_{1}^{\prime}}{f_{1}}d\nu=\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\quad\text{and}\quad\int\frac{g_{c}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}}{g_{c}}d\nu=0,

where for the last one, we use the fact that
g_{c}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}/g_{c}
is an odd function.

Lastly, for the term
V_{2}V_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{2}V_{3}]=\int\frac{f_{2}^{\prime\prime}g_{\delta}+2f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}^{\prime}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}\times\{f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}\}\times\{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}\}d\nu=0,

since

\displaystyle\int(f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime})d\nu=0.

Next turning to the expectations of
W_{i}V_{j}
, we want to show that
\mathbb{E}[W_{i}V_{j}]=0
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Making use of the previous results, we have a list of equations:

1.

Case
i=1,j=1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{1}V_{1}]~=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{1}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}+2f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}}\frac{f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}}f_{1}f_{2}g_{c}g_{\delta}d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{1}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}+2f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}}\{f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}\}f_{2}g_{\delta}d\nu=0,

2.

Case
i=1,j=2
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{1}V_{2}]~=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}}\frac{f_{2}^{\prime\prime}g_{\delta}+2f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}^{\prime}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}f_{1}f_{2}g_{c}g_{\delta}d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int\{f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}\}\{f_{2}^{\prime\prime}g_{\delta}+2f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}^{\prime}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}\}d\nu=0,

3.

Case
i=1,j=3
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{1}V_{3}]~=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{(f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime})}{f_{1}g_{c}}\times\frac{(f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime})}{f_{1}g_{c}}\times\frac{(f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime})}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}f_{1}f_{2}g_{c}g_{\delta}d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{(f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime})^{2}}{f_{1}g_{c}}\times\{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}\}d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\frac{J_{1}}{b^{2}}\bigg\}\int\{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}\}d\nu=0,

4.

Case
i=2,j=1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{2}V_{1}]~=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}\frac{f_{1}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}+2f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{1}g_{c}}f_{1}f_{2}g_{c}g_{\delta}d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int\{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}\}\{f_{1}^{\prime\prime}g_{c}+2f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}^{\prime}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime\prime}\}d\nu=0,

5.

Case
i=2,j=2
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{2}V_{2}]~=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}\frac{f_{2}^{\prime\prime}g_{\delta}+2f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}^{\prime}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}f_{1}f_{2}g_{c}g_{\delta}d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int\{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}\}\frac{f_{2}^{\prime\prime}g_{\delta}+2f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}^{\prime}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}f_{1}g_{c}d\nu=0,

6.

Case
i=2,j=3
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{2}V_{3}]~=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}\times\frac{(f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime})}{f_{1}g_{c}}\times\frac{(f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime})}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}f_{1}f_{2}g_{c}g_{\delta}d\nu

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int\frac{(f_{2}^{\prime}g_{\delta}+f_{2}g_{\delta}^{\prime})^{2}}{f_{2}g_{\delta}}\times\{f_{1}^{\prime}g_{c}+f_{1}g_{c}^{\prime}\}d\nu=0.

In summary, the diagonal entries of
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}\bm{\eta}^{\top}]
are equal to zero and thus the claim follows. This completes the proof of
Theorem

7
.

Appendix D
Proofs of Additional Results

This section collects the proofs of the results in
Appendix

A
.

D.1
Proof of
Corollary

3

We begin with an argument that proves that
\widehat{\tau}_{f}
is a consistent estimator of
\tau_{f}
under the conditions of
Corollary

3
. The first term of
\widehat{\tau}_{f}
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl[\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{j})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{j})\}\biggr)\biggr]^{2}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl(\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})\biggr)^{2}}_{:=(\mathrm{I})}-\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{j})-\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{j})\}\biggr)^{2}}_{:=(\mathrm{II})}.

Focusing on the term (I), by adding and subtracting
f(X_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})
, we have the identity

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I})~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{f(X_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-f(X_{i})\bigr\}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{f(X_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-f(X_{i})\bigr\}+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{f(X_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}

\displaystyle+~

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-f(X_{i})\bigr\}.

Under the conditions
\mathrm{Var}[f(X)]<\infty
and
\mathrm{Var}[\ell(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})]<\infty
, the law of large numbers yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{f(X_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\big\{f(X)-\ell_{1}(Y)\big\}^{2}\bigr].

On the other hand, Markov’s inequality along with the condition

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-f(X_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{n}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{2}(X_{1})-f(X_{1})\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]+\frac{n-\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{n}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{n})-f(X_{n})\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]\rightarrow 0

shows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})-f(X_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0.

Following the analysis in (
18
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y)-\ell_{1}(Y)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]\lesssim\frac{\mathbb{E}[\ell^{2}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})]}{n}\rightarrow 0,

which combined with Markov’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{\widehat{\ell}_{1}(Y_{i})-\ell_{1}(Y_{i})\bigr\}^{2}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}0.

The sums of cross-product terms in the expansion of (I) are shown to converge to zero in probability by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. Therefore, we can conclude that the term (I) converges to zero in probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
. We can similarly analyze the term (
\mathrm{II}
) and prove that
(\mathrm{II})\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\{\mathbb{E}[f(X)-\ell_{1}(Y)]\}^{2}
. Since convergence in probability is closed under addition, we in turn have
(\mathrm{I})-\mathrm{(II)}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Var}[f(X)-\ell_{1}(Y)]
. Moreover,
Arvesen, 1969
shows
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\sigma^{2}
under the finite second moment of
\ell
. Consequently, it follows that
\widehat{\tau}_{f}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\tau_{f}
.

Having these ingredients, we are ready to prove

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}}{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}1.

(32)

Once this claim holds, then the result of
Corollary

3
follows by the continuous mapping theorem as well as Slutsky’s theorem. In order to prove the ratio-consistency (
32
), we note that

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}}{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}-1\bigg|~=~

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}-\Lambda_{n,m,f}}{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{\widehat{\Lambda}_{n,m,f}-\Lambda_{n,m,f}}{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X\}]}\bigg|

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X\}]}\big|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}\big|+\frac{r^{2}m}{(n+m)\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X\}]}\big|\widehat{\tau}_{f}-\tau_{f}\big|

where step (i) uses the inequality
\Lambda_{n,m,f}\geq\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}\{\ell_{1}(Y)\,|\,X\}]>0
, which holds by
Lemma

2
and our condition, and step (ii) uses the triangular inequality. As shown before, we have
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\sigma^{2}
and
\widehat{\tau}_{f}\overset{p}{\longrightarrow}\tau_{f}
, which proves the claim (
32
). This completes the proof of
Corollary

3
.

D.2
Proof of
Proposition

5

Recall that for
\ell(y)=y
, the semi-supervised U-statistic is given as

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{cross}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{Y_{i}-\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})\}+\frac{1}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i}),

and denote its oracle version with
f(x)=\beta_{(2)}^{\top}x
as

\displaystyle U_{f}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{Y_{i}-f(X_{i})\}+\frac{1}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}f(X_{i}).

Then
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
and
U_{f}
are related as
U_{\mathrm{cross}}=U_{f}+R
where

\displaystyle R:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})\}-\frac{1}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{\mathrm{cross}}(X_{i})\}.

We prove
Proposition

5
by first establishing a Berry–Esseen bound for
U_{f}
and then dealing with the remainder term
R
through a similar argument used in non-asymptotic Slutsky’s theorem in
Lemma

4
.

Berry–Esseen bound for
U_{f}
.
It can be seen that
U_{f}-\psi
can be written as

\displaystyle U_{f}-\psi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\bigg\{\frac{1}{n}(Y_{i}-\psi)-\frac{m}{n(n+m)}f(X_{i})\bigg\}}_{:=V_{i}}+\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+m}\underbrace{\frac{1}{n+m}f(X_{i})}_{:=W_{i}},

where
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
and
W_{n+1},\ldots,W_{n+m}
are mutually independent. Since
U_{f}-\psi
is invariant to a location shift of
f
, we may assume that
\mathbb{E}[V_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[W_{i}]=0
without loss of generality, and compute the variance as

\displaystyle n^{-1}\Lambda_{n,m,f}~=~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Var}[V_{i}]+\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+m}\mathrm{Var}[W_{i}]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\biggl[\mathrm{Var}[Y]+\frac{m}{n+m}\big\{\mathrm{Var}[f(X)]-2\mathrm{Cov}[f(X),\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)]\big\}\biggr]

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{Var}[Y]-\frac{m}{n(n+m)}\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X)]+\frac{1}{n+m}\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)]

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X)],

where the first inequality is due to
Lemma

2
. On the other hand, the sum of the absolute third moments is bounded as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[|V_{i}|^{3}]+\sum_{i=n+1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}[|W_{i}|^{3}]

\displaystyle\lesssim~

\displaystyle n\times\biggl[\frac{1}{n^{3}}\mathbb{E}[|Y-\psi|^{3}]+\frac{m^{3}}{n^{3}(n+m)^{3}}\mathbb{E}[|f(X)|^{3}]\biggr]+\frac{m}{(n+m)^{3}}\mathbb{E}[|f(X)|^{3}]

\displaystyle\lesssim~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|Y-\psi|^{3}]+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|f(X)|^{3}].

Having these inequalities along with the moment conditions (i) and (ii) in
Proposition

5
, a Berry–Esseen bound for independent random variables (
Lemma

5
) yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}(U_{f}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}\leq t\biggr)-\Phi(t)\bigg|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}.

(33)

Control of the remainder term
R
.
Following the proof of
Lemma

4
, we may arrive at

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}\leq t\biggr)\leq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}(U_{f}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}\leq t\biggr)+\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2\pi}}+\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}|R|}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}>\epsilon\biggr),

which holds for any
\epsilon>0
. As shown before, the first term in the upper bound is of the order
1/\sqrt{n}
. We now prove that the last term satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}|R|}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}>\epsilon\biggr)\lesssim\epsilon^{-2}\frac{d}{n}+e^{-Cn},

(34)

for some positive number
C
. Therefore by choosing
\epsilon\asymp(d/n)^{1/3}
, we prove the desired claim that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}(U_{\mathrm{cross}}-\psi)}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}\leq t\biggr)\lesssim\biggl(\frac{d}{n}\biggr)^{1/3}.

In what follows, we show the claim (
34
). As explained in the main text, we have
\widehat{f}_{1}(x)=x^{\top}\widehat{\beta}_{(2)}
where
\widehat{\beta}=(\widehat{\beta}_{1},\widehat{\beta}_{(2)})^{\top}=(\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{X}})\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{Y}}
computed on
\mathcal{D}_{XY,1}
, and
\widehat{f}_{2}
is similarly defined using
\mathcal{D}_{XY,2}
. With
n_{0}=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,
n_{1}=n-n_{0}
and
\mathcal{I}:=\{n_{0}+1,\ldots,n\}\cup\{n+\lfloor m/2\rfloor+1,\ldots,n+m\}
, let us define

\displaystyle R_{1}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}-\frac{1}{n+m}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\},

and
R_{2}:=R-R_{1}
. By the inequality
\mathds{1}(|x+y|\geq t)\leq\mathds{1}(|x|\geq t/2)+\mathds{1}(|y|\geq t/2)
holding for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}|R|}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}>\epsilon\biggr)~\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}|R_{1}|}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}>\epsilon/2\biggr)+\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{\sqrt{n}|R_{2}|}{\sqrt{\smash[b]{\Lambda_{n,m,f}}}}>\epsilon/2\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{n}|R_{1}|\gtrsim\epsilon\bigr)+\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{n}|R_{2}|\gtrsim\epsilon\bigr),

where the last inequality holds due to
Lemma

2
and the condition (ii)
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X)]>C_{4}
. Given this inequality and by the symmetry between
\widehat{f}_{1}
and
\widehat{f}_{2}
, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sqrt{n}|R_{1}|\gtrsim\epsilon\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\biggl(\bigg|\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}\bigg|\gtrsim\epsilon/\sqrt{n}\biggr)\lesssim\epsilon^{-2}\frac{d}{n}+e^{-Cn}.

(35)

Proof of the claim in (
35
).
We now focus on the proof of inequality (
35
). Throughout the rest of the proof, we assume that
\mathbb{E}(X)=0
and
\mathrm{Var}(X)=\bm{I}_{d}
. This assumption can be made without loss of generality. In detail, note that
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
with
\widehat{f}_{1}
and
\widehat{f}_{2}
remains the same as
U_{\mathrm{cross}}
with location-shifted versions of
\widehat{f}_{1}
and
\widehat{f}_{2}
. Hence, without loss of generality, we can work with the centered versions of
\widehat{f}_{1}
and
\widehat{f}_{2}
, defined as

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{1}(x)-\mathbb{E}\{\widehat{f}_{1}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{1}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{f}_{2}(x)-\mathbb{E}\{\widehat{f}_{2}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{2}\},

respectively. Moreover, we note that these centered functions remain invariant under affine transformations. To illustrate this, introduce a matrix

\displaystyle G=\begin{bmatrix}1&\bm{0}\\
\mu&\Sigma^{1/2}\end{bmatrix}

where
\bm{0}
is the
d\times d
matrix having zero elements,
\mathbb{E}(X)=\mu
and
\mathrm{Var}(X)=\Sigma
. With the matrix
G
, we can express
\vec{X}
as
\vec{X}=G\vec{Z}
where
\vec{Z}^{\top}=[1\ Z^{\top}]
and
\vec{\bm{X}}=\vec{\bm{Z}}G^{\top}
. This allows us to establish a series of identities:

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{1}(x)-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{f}_{1}(X)\,|\,\widehat{f}_{1}]~=~

\displaystyle[0\ (x-\mu)^{\top}]\widehat{\beta}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle[0\ (x-\mu)^{\top}](G\vec{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{Z}}G^{\top})^{-1}G\vec{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\bm{Y}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle[0\ (x-\mu)^{\top}]\bigl(G^{\top}\bigr)^{-1}(\vec{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{Z}})^{-1}\vec{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\bm{Y}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle[0\ z^{\top}]G^{\top}\bigl(G^{\top}\bigr)^{-1}(\vec{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{Z}})^{-1}\vec{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\bm{Y}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle[0\ z^{\top}](\vec{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{Z}})^{-1}\vec{\bm{Z}}^{\top}\bm{Y},

where
z=x-\mu
. This allows us to assume
\mathbb{E}(X)=0
and
\mathrm{Var}(X)=\bm{I}_{d}
without loss of generality. Let
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(n^{-1}\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{X}})
denote the minimum eigenvalue of the matrix
n^{-1}\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{X}}
. Under the conditions of
Proposition

5
, Lemma
6
yields that there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(n^{-1}\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{X}})\leq C_{1}\}\geq 1-e^{C_{2}n}.

Therefore, defining the event
\mathcal{Q}:=\{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(n^{-1}\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{X}})>C_{1}\}
, the union bound along with Chebyshev’s inequality gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\bigg|\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}\bigg|\gtrsim\epsilon/\sqrt{n}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\bigg|\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}\bigg|\gtrsim\epsilon/\sqrt{n},\,\mathcal{Q}\biggr)+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{Q}^{c})

\displaystyle\lesssim~

\displaystyle\frac{n}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}\bigg)^{2}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})\biggr]+e^{-C_{2}n}.

Focusing on the expectation term above, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=n_{0}+1}^{n}\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}\bigg)^{2}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})\biggr]=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\{f(X_{1})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{1})\}^{2}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})\biggr].

To explain, note that
\widehat{\beta}_{(2)}
is independent of
\mathcal{D}_{XY,2}
and
\mathbb{E}(X)=0
. Therefore, for
X_{i},X_{j}\in\mathcal{D}_{XY,2}
and
i\neq j
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\{f(X_{i})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{i})\}\{f(X_{j})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{j})\}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})]=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{\top}\widehat{\beta}_{(2)}X_{2}^{\top}\widehat{\beta}_{(2)}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})]=0.

Next, note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\{f(X_{1})-\widehat{f}_{1}(X_{1})\}^{2}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})\bigr]~=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}}\mathbb{E}[(\beta_{(2)}-\widehat{\beta}_{(2)})^{\top}X_{1}X_{1}^{\top}(\beta_{(2)}-\widehat{\beta}_{(2)})\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|\beta_{(2)}-\widehat{\beta}_{(2)}\|_{2}^{2}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})\bigr]~\leq~\frac{1}{n_{1}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|\beta-\widehat{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})\bigr]

\displaystyle\lesssim~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{3}}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}^{-2}\bigl(n_{1}^{-1}\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}\vec{\bm{X}}\bigr)\|\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}(\bm{Y}-\vec{\bm{X}}\beta)\|^{2}_{2}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{Q})\Bigr]

\displaystyle\lesssim~

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{3}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}(\bm{Y}-\vec{\bm{X}}\beta)\|^{2}_{2}\bigr].

By writing
X_{0i}=1
for
i\in[n_{1}]
and
\beta^{\top}=(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{d})
,

\displaystyle\|\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}(\bm{Y}-\vec{\bm{X}}\beta)\|^{2}_{2}=\biggl\{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}\biggl(Y_{j}-\sum_{i=0}^{d}\beta_{i}X_{ji}\biggr)\biggr\}^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{d}\biggl\{\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}X_{jk}\biggl(Y_{j}-\sum_{i=0}^{d}\beta_{i}X_{ji}\biggr)\biggr\}^{2}.

Simply let
\delta_{j}=Y_{j}-\vec{X}^{\top}_{j}\beta
for
j\in[n_{1}]
. Since
\mathbb{E}[\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}(\bm{Y}-\vec{\bm{X}}\beta)]=0
, we have
\mathbb{E}(\delta_{j})=0
and
\mathbb{E}(\vec{X}_{j,(k)}\delta_{j})=0
for
j\in[n_{1}]
and
k\in[d]
. By the moment condition (iv), it holds that
\mathbb{E}(\delta_{j}^{2})<C_{6}
and
\mathbb{E}(\vec{X}_{j,(k)}^{2}\delta_{j}^{2})<C_{6}
for
k\in[d]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[n_{1}^{-2}\|\vec{\bm{X}}^{\top}(\bm{Y}-\vec{\bm{X}}\beta)\|^{2}_{2}\Bigr]\lesssim\frac{d}{n}.

This proves the inequality (
35
), and so completes the proof of
Proposition

5
.

D.3
Proof of
Proposition

6

We prove the lower bound and upper bound in order.

Lower bound.
We start by proving that
\mathsf{Risk}_{L,q}\leq\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]
. For this claim, we consider a similar strategy taken in
Wu and Yang, 2016
and
Neykov et al., 2021
that study minimax risks under Poisson sampling. In particular, by the minimax theorem such as
Strasser, 1985
and
Polyanskiy and Wu, 2023
, the minimax risk coincides with the Bayes risk using a least favorable prior. In particular, under the conditions (i), (ii) and (iii), we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]=\sup_{\pi}\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\int\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]\mathrm{d}\pi(\theta):=\sup_{\pi}\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)],

where
\pi
ranges over all prior distributions on
\Theta
. Fix a prior distribution
\pi
and consider an arbitrary estimator
\widehat{\theta}
on the action space
\widehat{\Theta}
. Moreover let
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(N=i)
be the normalized probability defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(N=i)=\frac{\mathbb{P}(N=i)}{\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}(N=j)},

where
q
is some fixed value in
(1/2,1)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]~=~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=i]\mathbb{P}(N=i)

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=i]\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(N=i)\Biggr\}\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}(N=j).

In general, there is no guarantee that the sequence of Bayes risks

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}:=\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=k]

is decreasing in
k
. To detour this hurdle, we define another estimator associated with
\widehat{\theta}
but satisfying the monotonicity property. Let
\widehat{\theta}_{k}
be the estimator
\widehat{\theta}
calculated based on the dataset
\{Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}\cup\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n+m}
if
k\geq 1
and
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n+m}
if
k=0
. Note that the Bayes risk of
\widehat{\theta}_{k}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{k},\theta)]
, is equivalent to
\alpha_{k}
. Let
\{\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}\}
be a sequence defined recursively as
\widetilde{\alpha}_{0}=\alpha_{0}
and
\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}=\min\{\widetilde{\alpha}_{j-1},\alpha_{j}\}
, and define another estimator
\widetilde{\theta}_{k}
as follows. First, let
\widetilde{\theta}_{0}=\widehat{\theta}_{0}
and, for each
1\leq k\leq\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor
, let

\displaystyle\widetilde{\theta}_{k}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\theta}_{k-1}\quad&\text{if $\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}=\widetilde{\alpha}_{k-1}$,}\\[5.0pt]
\widehat{\theta}_{k}\quad&\text{if $\widetilde{\alpha}_{k}<\widetilde{\alpha}_{k-1}$.}\end{cases}

On the other hand, if
k>\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor
, take
\widetilde{\theta}_{k}=\widehat{\theta}_{k}
. By construction, the Bayes risk of this recursively defined estimator satisfies

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}\geq\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\theta}_{N},\theta)\,|\,N=k]

and it is a non-increasing function of
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor\}
. Therefore, continuing from the previous inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]~\geq~

\displaystyle\Biggl\{\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\theta}_{N},\theta)\,|\,N=i]\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(N=i)\Biggr\}\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}(N=j)

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\geq}~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\theta}_{N},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor]\times\Bigl\{1-e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{4}}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{\geq}~

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor]\times\Bigl\{1-e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{4}}\Bigr\},

where step (i) uses the monotonicity property of
\widetilde{\theta}_{N}
as well as
Lemma

8
with
\rho=n^{q-1}
, and step (ii) follows by the definition of infimum.
By taking the supremum over
\pi
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]~\geq~

\displaystyle\sup_{\pi}\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor]\times\Bigl\{1-e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{4}}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor]\times\Bigl\{1-e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{4}}\Bigr\},

where the equality follows by the minimax theorem. Moreover, since the Bayes risk is no larger than the minimax risk and
\widehat{\theta}
was an arbitrary estimator, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]~\geq~

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\pi}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n+n^{q}\rfloor]\times\Bigl\{1-e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{4}}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathsf{Risk}_{L,q},

as desired.

Upper bound.
We next prove that
\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]\leq\mathsf{Risk}_{U,q}
. For this claim, recall that
N=\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\delta_{i}\sim\mathrm{Binomial}(n+m,\frac{n}{n+m})
, and define an event
\mathcal{A}=\{N\leq n-n^{q}\}
. Setting
\rho=n^{q-1}
for some fixed
q\in(1/2,1)
in Lemma
8
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A})\leq e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{2}}.

Let
\widehat{\theta}_{\star}
be an estimator that satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor]\leq\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor]+e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{2}}.

(36)

We also assume that the conditional risk of
\widehat{\theta}_{\star}
is monotone in
N
, satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\,|\,N=i]\leq\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor]\quad\text{for all $i>n-n^{q}+1$.}

(37)

If this monotonicity condition is violated, we modify
\widehat{\theta}_{\star}
in a way that it only uses
\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor
labeled data whenever
i>n-n^{q}+1
. This modified estimator satisfies both (
36
) and (
37
).

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, observe

\displaystyle\sup_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A})]\leq\sup_{\widehat{\theta},\theta}\{\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]\}^{1/2}\{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A})\}^{1/2}\leq\sup_{\widehat{\theta},\theta}\{\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]\}^{1/2}e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{4}}.

Using this together with the triangle inequality yields

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]~\leq~

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A})]+\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A}^{c})]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sup_{\widehat{\theta},\theta}\{\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]\}^{1/2}e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{4}}+\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A}^{c})].

Focusing on the second term above, observe that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A}^{c})]~=~

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta}\sum_{i=0}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\mathds{1}(N>n-n^{q})\,|\,N=i]\mathbb{P}(N=i)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta}\sum_{i=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\,|\,N=i]\mathbb{P}(N=i)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor}^{n+m}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\,|\,N=i]\mathbb{P}(N=i)

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{i})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor}^{n+m}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{\star},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor]\mathbb{P}(N=i)

\displaystyle\overset{(\mathrm{ii})}{\leq}~

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor]+e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{2}},

where step (i) uses our monotonicity condition for
\widehat{\theta}_{\star}
in (
37
), and step (ii) uses the condition for
\widehat{\theta}_{\star}
in (
36
). Putting things together yields the desired result

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]~\leq~

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\,|\,N=\lfloor n-n^{q}+1\rfloor]

\displaystyle+\sup_{\widehat{\theta},\theta}\{\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}^{2}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)]\}^{1/2}e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{4}}+e^{-\frac{n^{2q-1}}{2}}\leq\mathsf{Risk}_{U,q}.

D.4
Proof of
Proposition

7

Recall that a random vector
(X,Y)
from
P_{XY}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{mean}}
has the relationship
Y=X+\varepsilon
where
X\sim N(\delta,\sigma_{X}^{2})
and
\varepsilon\sim N(c,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})
are independent. The main idea of establishing the lower bound is to view the target parameter
\psi=\mathbb{E}[Y]
as a function of
c
and
\delta
, and apply the van Tree inequality (also called Bayesian Cram ́er–Rao lower bound). To apply the van Tree inequality, we need to compute the Fisher information of
\psi
. To this end, denoting the correlation between
X
and
Y
as
\rho:=\mathrm{Cov}(X,Y)/\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(X)\mathrm{Var}(Y)}
, we use the density formula of the conditional distribution of a multivariate Normal distribution to derive

\displaystyle Y\,|\,X=x\sim N\bigl(c+x,\ (1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\bigr).

We denote the conditional density of
Y\,|\,X=x
as
\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(\cdot\,|\,x,c)
and the density of
X
as
\phi_{X}(\cdot\,|\,\delta)
. Then the likelihood function of
(\delta,c)
becomes

\displaystyle L(\delta,c)~=~\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{Y\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0muX}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},c)\prod_{j=1}^{m+n}\phi_{X}(X_{j}\,|\,\delta).

By taking the logarithm of the likelihood function,

\displaystyle\log L(\delta,c):=\widetilde{\ell}(\delta,c)=

\displaystyle-\frac{n}{2}\log\bigl(2\pi(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\bigr)-\frac{1}{2(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-X_{i}-c)^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{m+n}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{X}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m+n}(X_{i}-\delta)^{2}

and taking derivatives of
\widetilde{\ell}
with respect to
(\delta,c)
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{\ell}}{\partial\delta}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{m+n}(X_{i}-\delta)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial\widetilde{\ell}}{\partial c}=\frac{1}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-X_{i}-c).

The Fisher information matrix of
(\delta,c)
is then given as

\displaystyle I(\delta,c)=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{m+n}{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}\end{bmatrix}.

(38)

Now consider a uniform prior distribution of
(c,\delta)
whose density is given as

\displaystyle q(c,\delta)=\underbrace{\frac{1}{K}\cos^{2}\biggl(\frac{\pi c}{2K}\biggr)\mathds{1}(-K\leq c\leq K)}_{=\ q_{1}(c)}\times\underbrace{\frac{1}{K}\cos^{2}\biggl(\frac{\pi\delta}{2K}\biggr)\mathds{1}(-K\leq\delta\leq K)}_{=\ q_{2}(\delta)}.

Note that each marginal
q_{i}
is differentiable on
[-K,K]
and vanishes on the boundary. Moreover,

\displaystyle\int\cdots\int\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}L(\delta,c)dy_{1}\cdots dx_{m+n}=\int\cdots\int\frac{\partial}{\partial c}L(\delta,c)dy_{1}\cdots dx_{m+n}=0,

which allows us to apply the (multivariate) van Trees inequality
(e.g.,
Polyanskiy and Wu, 2023
, Theorem 29.3)
. In particular, following the proof of
Polyanskiy and Wu, 2023
, the Fisher information matrix of the prior distribution
I(q)
can be computed as

\displaystyle I(q)=\mathrm{diag}\Biggl\{\int_{-K}^{K}\frac{q^{\prime}(\delta)^{2}}{q(\delta)}d\nu,\int_{-K}^{K}\frac{q^{\prime}(c)^{2}}{q(c)}dc\Biggr\}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{K^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}.

Noting that
g(\delta,c):=c+\delta=\psi
and
\bigl(\frac{\partial g}{\partial c},\frac{\partial g}{\partial\delta}\bigr)=(1,1)
, the Bayes risk is then lower bounded as

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\psi}}\int_{-K}^{K}\int_{-K}^{K}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\widehat{\psi}-g(\delta,c)\bigr)^{2}\bigr]q_{1}(c)q_{2}(\delta)dcd\nu

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&1\end{pmatrix}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[I(\delta,c)]+I(q)\bigr)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\biggl(\frac{m+n}{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{K^{2}}\biggr)^{-1}+\biggl(\frac{n}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{K^{2}}\biggr)^{-1}.

Since the value of
K
is arbitrary and the Bayes risk does not exceed the minimax risk, we may conclude that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\psi}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{mean}}}n\mathbb{E}_{P}\bigl[(\widehat{\psi}-\psi)^{2}\bigr]~\geq~

\displaystyle(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{n+m}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X)]+\frac{n}{n+m}\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)]

as desired. This completes the proof of
Proposition

7
.

Remark 3
.

Based on the expression (
38
), we can deduce that the Fisher information of the parameter
\psi=\mathbb{E}[Y]
is
I(\psi)=\bigl(\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{m+n}+\frac{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{n}\bigr)^{-1}=\bigl(\frac{1}{m+n}\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)]+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X)]\bigr)^{-1}
. Therefore the Cramér–Rao lower bound yields that any unbiased estimator
\hat{\psi}
of
\psi
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(\hat{\psi})\geq I^{-1}(\psi)=\frac{1}{m+n}\mathrm{Var}[\mathbb{E}(Y\,|\,X)]+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\,|\,X)].

Consequently, the oracle mean estimator presented in
Section

2.1
:

\displaystyle U^{\star}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i})\big\}+\frac{1}{n+m}\sum_{i=1}^{n+m}\mathbb{E}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i})

is efficient whose variance achieves this lower bound.
